:
\mathfrak{z}_{1}\leftarrow\prod_{k\in[m]}{\mathfrak{z}}^{(k)}_{1}
;
\mathfrak{z}_{2}\leftarrow e({\boldsymbol{\tilde{S}}}_{j}^{\prime},yf_{2}^{{\boldsymbol{\tilde{c}}}_{j}^{\prime}})/e(f_{1}g_{1}^{{\boldsymbol{v}}_{j}^{\prime}}h_{1}^{{\boldsymbol{\tilde{r}}}_{j}^{\prime}},f_{2})

\mathfrak{g}_{1}\leftarrow e({\boldsymbol{\tilde{S}}}_{j}^{\prime},f_{2})
;
\mathfrak{g}_{2}\leftarrow e(g_{1},yf_{2}^{{\boldsymbol{\tilde{c}}}_{j}^{\prime}})

\mathcal{Q}[\![res]\!]\leftarrow\mathsf{DPK}((\mathfrak{g}_{1},\mathfrak{g}_{2},\mathfrak{h}_{1},\mathfrak{h}_{2},\mathfrak{h}_{3},\mathfrak{z}_{1},\mathfrak{z}_{2}),p_{\mathsf{BBS+}_{j}},

(\mathcal{M}_{k}[\![{\boldsymbol{b}}^{(k)}_{{\boldsymbol{S}}_{j}},{\boldsymbol{b}}^{(k)}_{{\boldsymbol{c}}_{j}},{\boldsymbol{b}}^{(k)}_{{\boldsymbol{r}}_{j}},{\delta}^{(k)}_{0},{\delta}^{(k)}_{1},{\delta}^{(k)}_{2}]\!])_{k\in[m]},\mathcal{Q}[\![]\!])

where
p_{\mathsf{BBS+}_{j}}:=(\mathfrak{z}_{1}=\mathfrak{h}_{2}^{{\boldsymbol{b}}_{{\boldsymbol{S}}_{j}}}\mathfrak{h}_{3}^{\delta_{0}}\wedge 1_{\mathbb{G}_{T}}=\mathfrak{z}_{1}^{{\boldsymbol{-b}}_{{\boldsymbol{c}}_{j}}}\mathfrak{h}_{2}^{\delta_{1}}\mathfrak{h}_{3}^{\delta_{2}}\wedge

\mathfrak{z}_{2}=\mathfrak{g}_{1}^{{\boldsymbol{b}}_{{\boldsymbol{c}}_{j}}}\mathfrak{g}_{2}^{{\boldsymbol{b}}_{{\boldsymbol{S}}_{j}}}\mathfrak{h}_{1}^{{\boldsymbol{b}}_{{\boldsymbol{r}}_{j}}}\mathfrak{h}_{2}^{\delta_{1}})

\mathcal{Q}:

if

:

J^{*}\leftarrow J^{*}\cup\{j\}

endfor

output

\mathcal{Q}[\![J^{*}]\!]

Figure 9
.
Protocol
{\mathsf{DB}\text{-}\mathsf{RSM}}
(see summary in Figure
6(c)
).

Figure
9
shows our
{\mathsf{DB}\text{-}\mathsf{RSM}}
protocol. Note that to obtain BBS+ signatures on committed values, knowledge of commitment openings must be first shown. For this,
\mathcal{Q}
checks the NIZKs
{\boldsymbol{\rho_{\gamma}}}_{i}
uploaded by the senders for each
i\in I
.
\mathcal{Q}
then sends valid BBS+ quasi-signatures
{\boldsymbol{\hat{\sigma}}}_{i}
on
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{i}
for
({\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{i})_{i\in I}
and invalid ones for
({\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{i})_{i\not\in I}
. It encrypts each component
({\boldsymbol{S}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{c}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{\hat{r}}}_{i})
of
{\boldsymbol{\hat{\sigma}}}_{i}
independently, using
\mathsf{E}_{\mathsf{EG}}^{\mathsf{th}}
for
{\boldsymbol{S}}_{i}
and
\mathsf{E}_{\mathsf{Pa}}^{\mathsf{th}}
for
{\boldsymbol{c}}_{i}
and
{\boldsymbol{\hat{r}}}_{i}
, to create encrypted quasi-signatures
{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}_{{\boldsymbol{\hat{\sigma}}}_{i}}:=({\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}_{{\boldsymbol{S}}_{i}},{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}_{{\boldsymbol{c}}_{i}},{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}_{{\boldsymbol{\hat{r}}}_{i}})
. Using
{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}_{{\boldsymbol{\hat{r}}}_{i}}
and the sender-uploaded encryptions
{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}_{{\boldsymbol{r}}_{i}}
, encrypted BBS+ signatures on the committed values are derived by homomorphically adding the commitment randomness
{\boldsymbol{r}}_{i}
to the signature’s
{\boldsymbol{\hat{r}}}_{i}
component. Thus, encrypted (valid and invalid) BBS+ signatures
{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}_{{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{i}}:=({\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}_{{\boldsymbol{S}}_{i}},{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}_{{\boldsymbol{c}}_{i}},{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}_{{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{r}}}_{i}}:={\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}_{{\boldsymbol{\hat{r}}}_{i}}{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}_{{\boldsymbol{r}}_{i}})_{i\in[n]}
are obtained next to each commitment
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{i}
in the input list.

To obtain blinded BBS+ signatures on plaintext values in the permuted list
\boldsymbol{v}^{\prime}
, each component
(\boldsymbol{\epsilon_{S}},\boldsymbol{\epsilon_{c}},\boldsymbol{\epsilon_{\mathsf{r}}})
of the BBS+ signature is re-encrypted individually using encryption schemes
(\mathsf{E}_{\mathsf{EG}}^{\mathsf{th}},\mathsf{E}_{\mathsf{Pa}}^{\mathsf{th}},\mathsf{E}_{\mathsf{Pa}}^{\mathsf{th}})
respectively and shuffled in the forward direction. The permuted and re-encrypted signatures
(\boldsymbol{\epsilon_{S}}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{\epsilon_{c}}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{\epsilon_{\mathsf{r}}}^{\prime})
are individually blinded to obtain
(\boldsymbol{\tilde{\epsilon_{S}}}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{\tilde{\epsilon_{c}}}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{\tilde{\epsilon_{\mathsf{r}}}}^{\prime})
, which are individually threshold-decrypted to obtain blinded BBS+ signatures
{\boldsymbol{\tilde{\sigma}}}_{j}^{\prime}:=({\boldsymbol{\tilde{S}}}_{j}^{\prime},{\boldsymbol{\tilde{c}}}_{j}^{\prime},{\boldsymbol{\tilde{r}}}_{j}^{\prime})
alongside
{\boldsymbol{v}}_{j}^{\prime}
.

Note that
\mathsf{E}_{\mathsf{Pa}}^{\mathsf{th}}
used for encrypting
\boldsymbol{\epsilon_{c}}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{\epsilon_{\mathsf{r}}}^{\prime}
is homomorphic in group
\mathbb{Z}_{N}
, which induces addition modulo
N
in the plaintext space, not modulo
q
. Thus, blinding by blinding factors drawn from
\mathbb{Z}_{q}
would not be perfectly hiding. To circumvent this issue, we follow an approach similar to
(
Gennaro et al. 2016
;
Gennaro and Goldfeder 2018
)
. First, we ensure that
N
is much larger than
q
so that all additions remain integer additions and do not wrap around
N
(this is anyway the case since
N
is usually a 2048-bit modulus,
q
is of
254
bits and we perform a small number of homomorphic additions per ciphertext). Second, we pad blinding factors
{\boldsymbol{b_{c}}}_{j},{\boldsymbol{b_{r}}}_{j}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}
for
{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}_{{\boldsymbol{c}}_{j}}^{\prime},{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}_{{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{r}}}_{j}}^{\prime}
by much larger offsets
q{\boldsymbol{\chi}}_{{\boldsymbol{c}}_{j}},q{\boldsymbol{\chi}}_{{\boldsymbol{r}}_{j}}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q(q-1)}
so that the padded blinding factors
{\boldsymbol{b_{c}}}_{j}+q{\boldsymbol{\chi}}_{{\boldsymbol{c}}_{j}},{\boldsymbol{b_{r}}}_{j}+q{\boldsymbol{\chi}}_{{\boldsymbol{r}}_{j}}
are identically distributed to uniform samples from
\mathbb{Z}_{q^{2}}
and provide an almost perfect integer blinding for the messages
{\boldsymbol{c}}_{\pi(j)}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}
and
{\boldsymbol{\hat{r}}}_{\pi(j)}+{\boldsymbol{r}}_{\pi(j)}\in\mathbb{Z}_{2q}
. The offsets are removed by reducing decryptions
{\boldsymbol{\tilde{c}}}_{j}^{\prime\prime},{\boldsymbol{\tilde{r}}}_{j}^{\prime\prime}
of
{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}_{{\boldsymbol{c}}_{j}}^{\prime},{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}_{{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{r}}}_{j}}^{\prime}
modulo
q
.

In stage 2, for each
j\in J
, a DPK is given that proves that the mix-servers know shares of
{\boldsymbol{b}}_{{\boldsymbol{S}}_{j}},{\boldsymbol{b}}_{{\boldsymbol{c}}_{j}},{\boldsymbol{b}}_{{\boldsymbol{r}}_{j}}
such that
({\boldsymbol{\tilde{S}}}_{j}^{\prime}g_{1}^{-{\boldsymbol{b}}_{{\boldsymbol{S}}_{j}}},

{\boldsymbol{\tilde{c}}}_{j}^{\prime}-{\boldsymbol{b}}_{{\boldsymbol{c}}_{j}}\bmod q,

{\boldsymbol{\tilde{r}}}_{j}^{\prime}-{\boldsymbol{b}}_{{\boldsymbol{r}}_{j}}\bmod q)
is a valid BBS+ signature on message
{\boldsymbol{v}}_{j}^{\prime}
under
\mathcal{Q}
’s verification key. The actual DPK used in Figure
9
is designed to follow the format of Section
3.1.5
. Since each mix-server knows shares of
{\boldsymbol{b_{S}}}_{j},{\boldsymbol{b_{c}}}_{j},{\boldsymbol{b_{r}}}_{j},\delta_{0}
and gets shares of
\delta_{1}
and
\delta_{2}
via Beaver’s
\mathsf{Mult}
algorithm, these DPKs can be given efficiently.

4.3.1.
HBC to malicious security

Now we highlight steps to maintain secrecy even when the corrupted parties maliciously deviate from the protocol (see Appendix
B
for detailed steps). Any party that publishes an encryption - senders during
\mathsf{Enc}
, the querier, or the mix-servers during
\mathsf{Mix}
or
\mathsf{BTraceIn}
/
\mathsf{BTraceOut}
protocols - must provide proofs of knowledge of underlying plaintexts. This is to avoid attacks where re-encryptions of honest senders’ ciphertexts are published to get them decrypted. Moreover, before participating in threshold decryption protocols, honest mix-servers must verify that all encryptions are correctly created and consistently shuffled using the same permutation in
{\mathsf{DB}\text{-}\mathsf{RSM}}
as in
\mathsf{Mix}
and the inverse permutation in
{\mathsf{DB}\text{-}\mathsf{SM}}
(say, using techniques of
(
Wikström 2009
;
Terelius and Wikström 2010
)
). Mix-servers should also verify that the querier’s signatures are valid for all elements in the requested set and invalid for the complement set. The DPKs can be converted using Fiat-Shamir heuristic
(
Fiat and Shamir 1986
)
.

5.
Security analysis

Theorem 1 (Completeness).

Let
\Pi_{\mathsf{TM}}
be the protocol of Figure
7
.
\Pi_{\mathsf{TM}}
is complete (Definition
2
). (Proof in Appendix
C.1
).

Theorem 2 (Soundness).

Under the DL assumption in
\mathbb{G}_{1}
and
n
-SDH assumption in
(\mathbb{G}_{1},\mathbb{G}_{2})

(
Boneh and Boyen 2004
)
,
\Pi_{\mathsf{TM}}
is sound (Definition
3
).

Proof sketch (full proof in Appendix
C.2
):
If an adversary won
\mathsf{Exp}_{\mathsf{soundness}}
then
\mathcal{Q}
must have output either 1) a wrong
{\boldsymbol{c}}_{I^{*}}
in a
\mathsf{BTraceIn}(I,J)
query; or 2) a wrong
{\boldsymbol{v}}_{J^{*}}^{\prime}
in a
\mathsf{BTraceOut}(I,J)
query. Case 1 implies that either
a)

{\boldsymbol{c}}_{I^{*}}
included a
{\boldsymbol{c}}_{i}\in{\boldsymbol{c}}_{I}
not encrypting a plaintext in
{\boldsymbol{v}}_{J}^{\prime}
or
b)
it excluded a
{\boldsymbol{c}}_{i}\in{\boldsymbol{c}}_{I}
encrypting a plaintext in
{\boldsymbol{v}}_{J}^{\prime}
. Case 1a directly reduces to breaking the soundness of DB-SM for
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{i}
against set
{\boldsymbol{v}}_{J}^{\prime}
. Further, since each
{\boldsymbol{c}}_{i}\in{\boldsymbol{c}}_{I}\setminus{\boldsymbol{c}}_{I^{*}}
must be in
{\boldsymbol{c}}_{I_{\mathsf{c}}^{*}}
for
\mathcal{Q}
to not abort, case 1b also reduces to breaking the soundness of DB-SM (because if
{\boldsymbol{c}}_{i}\not\in{\boldsymbol{c}}_{I^{*}}
encrypts a plaintext in
{\boldsymbol{v}}_{J}^{\prime}
, it does not encrypt a plaintext in
{\boldsymbol{v}}_{[n]\setminus J}^{\prime}
due to distinctness of
{\boldsymbol{v}}_{j}^{\prime}
s, but
{\boldsymbol{c}}_{i}\in{\boldsymbol{c}}_{I_{\mathsf{c}}^{*}}
). Case 2 similarly reduces to breaking the soundness of DB-RSM.

Soundness of
{\mathsf{DB}\text{-}\mathsf{SM}}
for
I,J
:
The DPK proves that
(\mathcal{M}_{k})_{k\in[m]}
know blinding factors to unblind
{\boldsymbol{\tilde{\sigma}}}_{i}
to a valid BB signature on the value
{\boldsymbol{v}}_{i}
committed by
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{i}
under
\mathcal{Q}
’s fresh public key. Since
\mathcal{Q}
issued valid signatures only for plaintexts in
{\boldsymbol{v}}_{J}^{\prime}
, passing the DPK if
{\boldsymbol{v}}_{i}\not\in{\boldsymbol{v}}_{J}^{\prime}
requires
(\mathcal{M}_{k})_{k\in[m]}
to forge a BB signature under
\mathcal{Q}
’s public key, which is hard under the stated assumptions.

Soundness of
{\mathsf{DB}\text{-}\mathsf{RSM}}
for
I,J
:
The DPK proves that
(\mathcal{M}_{k})_{k\in[m]}
know blinding factors to unblind
{\boldsymbol{\tilde{\sigma}}}_{j}^{\prime}
to a valid BBS+ signature on
{\boldsymbol{v}}_{j}^{\prime}
under
\mathcal{Q}
’s fresh public key. Since
\mathcal{Q}
issued valid signatures only for commitments in
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{I}
, deriving a signature on a plaintext not committed in any commitment in
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{I}
reduces to breaking the soundness of the BBS+ scheme for obtaining signatures on committed values, which is hard under the stated assumptions.

Theorem 3 (Secrecy).

Under the IND-CPA security of
\mathsf{E}
, the DDH assumption in
\mathbb{G}_{1}
and the DCR assumption
(
Paillier 1999
)
,
\Pi_{\mathsf{TM}}
protects secrecy (Definition
4
) against HBC adversaries in the random oracle model.

Proof sketch (full proof in Appendix
C.3
):
We need to show that for any pair of values
v_{i_{0}},v_{i_{1}}
, no PPT adversary controlling
(\mathcal{M}_{k})_{k\not\eq k^{*}}
,
(S_{i})_{i\not\in\{i_{0},i_{1}\}}
and
\mathcal{Q}
can distinguish between world
0
where
S_{i_{0}}
sends
v_{i_{0}}
and
S_{i_{1}}
sends
v_{i_{1}}
and world
1
where this order is reversed, if the assert conditions in all
\mathsf{OTraceIn}
and
\mathsf{OTraceOut}
calls of
\mathsf{Exp}_{\mathsf{secrecy}}
are respected (and all adversarial parties are honest-but-curious). We do this by simplifying world
b
for each
b\in\{0,1\}
by the following sequence of indistinguishability arguments:

-

For each
i\in\{i_{0},i_{1}\}
, NIZK
{\boldsymbol{\rho_{\gamma}}}_{i}
can be simulated, shares
{\boldsymbol{v}}^{(k)}_{i},{\boldsymbol{r}}^{(k)}_{i}
given to
(\mathcal{M}_{k})_{k\neq k^{*}}
can be replaced by random elements drawn from
\mathbb{Z}_{q}
, and encryptions
{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{ev}}}^{(k^{*})}_{i}
,
{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{er}}}^{(k^{*})}_{i}
can be replaced by encryptions of
0
by the IND-CPA security of
\mathsf{E}
.

-

Threshold decryption protocols for
\mathsf{E}_{\mathsf{EG}}^{\mathsf{th}}
and
\mathsf{E}_{\mathsf{Pa}}^{\mathsf{th}}
do not reveal any information beyond the decryption output, which can be obtained by permuting the list of input values, where
S_{i_{0}}
and
S_{i_{1}}
’s input values are
v_{i_{b}}
and
v_{i_{1-b}}
respectively. With the decryption oracles now eliminated, all
\mathsf{E}_{\mathsf{EG}}^{\mathsf{th}}
and
\mathsf{E}_{\mathsf{Pa}}^{\mathsf{th}}
encryptions/re-encryptions can be replaced by encryptions of dummy values, by the IND-CPA security of
\mathsf{E}_{\mathsf{EG}}^{\mathsf{th}}
and
\mathsf{E}_{\mathsf{Pa}}^{\mathsf{th}}
under the DDH and DCR assumptions respectively.

-

The only information leaked during a DPK for
S_{i_{0}}/S_{i_{1}}
’s commitment/plaintext is whether the DPK passed or not, but the assert conditions ensure that this information is the same in both the worlds. Thus, these DPKs can be simulated by a ZK simulator that does not know which specific world it is in.

-

For
i\in\{i_{0},i_{1}\}
,
{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{ev}}}^{(k^{*})}_{i}
,
{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{er}}}^{(k^{*})}_{i}
can be replaced by encryptions of
0
since the encrypted values
{\boldsymbol{v}}^{(k^{*})}_{i},{\boldsymbol{r}}^{(k^{*})}_{i}
are not used anywhere anymore (they were used in the DPKs/encryptions earlier). Similarly,
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{i_{0}},{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{i_{1}}
can now be replaced with commitments of
0
.

-

The blinded signatures corresponding to
S_{i_{0}}
or
S_{i_{1}}
’s commitments or plaintexts during
{\mathsf{DB}\text{-}\mathsf{SM}}
and
{\mathsf{DB}\text{-}\mathsf{RSM}}
can now be replaced with random group elements because they are not used anywhere anymore and the blinding factors chosen by
\mathcal{M}_{k^{*}}
are random (note: for the
c,r
components of the BBS+ signature, this holds because of flooding with large blinding factors).

The two worlds obtained now are indistinguishable because now only the mixnet output list
\boldsymbol{v}^{\prime}
depends on
v_{i_{0}}
and
v_{i_{1}}
and this list is identically distributed in the two worlds because of the uniformly chosen permutation
{\pi}^{(k^{*})}
by
\mathcal{M}_{k^{*}}
.

Theorem 4 (Output secrecy).

Under the same assumptions as Theorem
3
,
\Pi_{\mathsf{TM}}
protects output secrecy (Definition
5
). (Proof in Appendix
C.4
).

In Appendix
C.5
, we also sketch a proof that after applying the additional steps mentioned in Section
4.3.1
and Appendix
B
, our construction protects secrecy against general malicious adversaries.

5.1.
Privacy risk analysis of query outputs

Theorem
3
guarantees that a traceable mixnet does not reveal any information beyond the output of the
\mathsf{BTraceIn}/\mathsf{BTraceOut}
queries. To evaluate the privacy risk impact of these outputs themselves, we provide a mechanism in Appendix
D
to statically analyse information leaked by a given set of queries. Specifically, given a set
Q
of proposed TraceIn/TraceOut queries in an application, the mechanism outputs information potentially leaked by them:
a)
for each
i\in[n]
, the smallest set
J_{\mathsf{min}_{i}}
representing potential plaintexts that
{\boldsymbol{c}}_{i}
might encrypt and
b)
for each
j\in[n]
, the smallest set
I_{\mathsf{min}_{j}}
representing potential ciphertexts that
{\boldsymbol{v}}_{j}^{\prime}
might decrypt from (
J_{\mathsf{min}_{i}}=[n]
denotes that queries in
Q
reveal no additional information about
{\boldsymbol{c}}_{i}
; likewise for
I_{\mathsf{min}_{j}}
). The
J_{\mathsf{min}}/I_{\mathsf{min}}
information can then directly be used to analyse application-level security.

6.
Implementation and benchmarks

We implemented a proof-of-concept for our traceable mixnet construction, with the primary goal of evaluating its runtime query performance. Given this focus, we mainly implemented the
{\mathsf{DB}\text{-}\mathsf{SM}}
and
{\mathsf{DB}\text{-}\mathsf{RSM}}
protocols. This allows us to directly estimate the total time for
\mathsf{BTraceIn}
or
\mathsf{BTraceOut}
queries as that of two
{\mathsf{DB}\text{-}\mathsf{SM}}
or
{\mathsf{DB}\text{-}\mathsf{RSM}}
invocations. However, an optimisation where the
{\mathsf{DB}\text{-}\mathsf{SM}}
calls for
J
and
[n]\setminus J
(similarly for
{\mathsf{DB}\text{-}\mathsf{RSM}}
) are combined to a single call as follows considerably improves this estimate:
\mathcal{Q}
generates signature key pairs
x,y
for
J
and
x_{\mathsf{c}},y_{\mathsf{c}}
for
[n]\setminus J
and sends signatures using key
x_{\mathsf{c}}
for
j\not\in J
instead of invalid signatures, thus avoiding extraneous shuffling/decryption of invalid signatures.

We used standard threshold ElGamal encryption
(
Desmedt 1994
)
for
\mathsf{E}_{\mathsf{EG}}^{\mathsf{th}}
and Damgård et al.’s
(
Damgård et al. 2010
)
optimised threshold Paillier encryption for
\mathsf{E}_{\mathsf{Pa}}^{\mathsf{th}}
.
2
2

2

We slightly simplified
(
Damgård et al. 2010
)
by skipping the factorial trick used for general
t
-out-of-
m
threshold cryptography in RSA groups, focusing on the
m
-out-of-
m
case.
For key generation, we implemented a simplified protocol where a trusted dealer distributes key shares to the mix-servers. Secure distributed key generation is trivial for
\mathsf{E}_{\mathsf{EG}}^{\mathsf{th}}
but requires a special protocol
(
Damgård and Koprowski 2001
)
for
\mathsf{E}_{\mathsf{Pa}}^{\mathsf{th}}
. However, our simplification is justified as key generation is a one-time setup step that can be executed ahead of time and does not affect runtime query performance.

We also implemented all the steps mentioned in Section
4.3.1
and Appendix
B
to evaluate performance in the realistic malicious setting. We used the following techniques to optimise these steps: 1) standard
\Sigma
-protocol techniques to let senders efficiently prove knowledge of their uploaded ciphertexts and mix-servers prove knowledge of blinding factors during homomorphic blinding; 2) batch-verification techniques of
(
Bellare et al. 1998
;
Ferrara et al. 2009
)
to let each mix-server efficiently verify the querier’s signatures/quasi-signatures and the correctness of other mix-servers’ decryption shares during threshold decryption; and 3)
permutation commitment
-based techniques of
(
Wikström 2009
;
Terelius and Wikström 2010
)
to let each mix-server efficiently prove that they created their shuffles consistently during
\mathsf{Mix}
,
{\mathsf{DB}\text{-}\mathsf{SM}}
and
{\mathsf{DB}\text{-}\mathsf{RSM}}
.

We implemented all sender proofs of knowledge required to achieve our soundness and secrecy requirements. However, we did not implement proofs of well-formedness of input ciphertexts (Section
2
): proofs that
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{i}
commit the same value as encrypted by
{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}_{i}
and that
{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}_{i}
,
{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}_{r_{i}}
encrypt values in the range
[0,q]
.
3
3

3

Senders do not need to prove distinctness of their encrypted messages because duplicate values can be detected at the mixnet output and the offending senders could be identified (by, say, a
\mathsf{BTraceIn}
query) on demand.
This is primarily due to our focus on runtime query performance and because these costs are incurred by individual senders and mix-servers as and when senders send their data. Our proofs of knowledge incur
\sim
0.15 seconds cost per-sender for both the sender and the mix-servers. With standard
\Sigma
-protocol techniques and FO commitments
(
Fujisaki and Okamoto 1997
)
for proving equality of committed and encrypted values and
(
Boudot 2000
;
Lindell 2017
)
for range proofs, the total time is expected to remain <1 s per sender.

Finally, we did not implement the authenticated broadcast channel. Since our uploaded datasets are moderate in size and the round complexity is small, we do not expect this to be a bottleneck.

Our implementation
(
Agrawal et al. 2023a
)
is based on the Charm library
(
Akinyele et al. 2013
)
with the PBC backend
(
Lynn 2013
)
for pairing operations. We chose the BN254 curve
(
Barreto and Naehrig 2006
;
Johnson 2021
)
to instantiate pairing groups
(\mathbb{G}_{1},

\mathbb{G}_{2},

\mathbb{G}_{T})
, which gives a group order
q
of 254 bits.
4
4

4

With NFS attacks
(
Kim and Barbulescu 2016
)
, the security of BN254 curves has dropped to 100-110 bits
(
Menezes et al. 2016
;
Edgington 2023
)
. We are limited to BN254 because of our chosen Charm library, but we estimate that the overall performance hit in per-mix-server time on switching to a more secure curve BLS12-381
(
Edgington 2023
)
is ¡1.3x, given that BLS12-381 operations are ¡2x slower than BN254
(
Consensys 2021
)
and that curve operations predominantly only affect our stage 2 DPKs.
We attempted to minimise the number of pairing operations wherever possible and used pre-computation of powers of fixed bases to speed-up exponentiation operations.

Figure 10
.
Performance of
{\mathsf{DB}\text{-}\mathsf{SM}}
and
{\mathsf{DB}\text{-}\mathsf{RSM}}
(
n
: number of input ciphertexts,
m
: number of mix-servers).
\mathcal{M}_{k}
and
\mathcal{Q}
denote per-mix-server and querier times respectively; collab-zkSNARK denotes estimated per-prover times in collaborative zkSNARKs
(
Ozdemir and Boneh 2022
)
.

We ran all our benchmarks on an Intel(R) Xeon(R) W-1270 CPU @ 3.40GHz with 64 GB RAM on a single core. Figure
10
shows the performance of our
{\mathsf{DB}\text{-}\mathsf{SM}}/{\mathsf{DB}\text{-}\mathsf{RSM}}
implementation in the worst case when
I=J=[n]
, with the overall mix-server and querier times for different
n
and
m
at the top and the detailed breakdown for
n=10000
ciphertexts and
m=4
mix-servers at the bottom. All reported values are averages over 3 runs. The deviation from the average in any run is <1.5%. We report per-mix-server times, which accurately capture real-world latencies as the heavy operations like proofs-of-shuffle, homomorphic blinding, threshold decryption and stage 2 DPKs can be run in parallel by the mix-servers. The only sequential operation is re-encryption, but it is a negligible fraction of other steps. We also report timings for the HBC case to highlight the overhead introduced by the malicious security steps.

Our ZKPs are practical for offline batch processing tasks, finishing within an hour and requiring moderate amount of data to be published for
10000
ciphertexts. They scale linearly with
n
. The scale-up with
m
is constant for the HBC mix-server time and for the querier, but in the malicious case, each mix-server needs to verify other mix-servers’ output, which leads to a
\sim
1.4x increase from
m=2
to
m=4
. Our main bottlenecks are expensive pairing and exponentiation computations in our DPKs and expensive Paillier operations in
{\mathsf{DB}\text{-}\mathsf{RSM}}
. The additional proofs for malicious security add an overhead of roughly 1.5-2x over the HBC case where these steps are skipped.

There exists a high degree of task parallelism in our construction, since DPKs in stage 2 are independent of each other and stage 1 operations incur at most constant communication overhead. Thus, we expect significant speedups if each mix-server and the querier are given multiple cores. With a moderate parallel cluster of 100 nodes, recovery for an election with
10^{6}
votes can thus be performed within a few hours. Further, if the signer in stage 1 of our ZKPs could be a separate trusted entity different than the querier and pre-sign all set entries, then our ZKPs become completely noninteractive and only incur stage 2 costs for verification.

Comparison

The only technique comparable to our distributed setting is collaborative zkSNARKs
(
Ozdemir and Boneh 2022
)
. We indirectly estimate our performance against them by employing a thumbrule given by
(
Ozdemir and Boneh 2022
)
that the per-prover time in a collaborative zkSNARK, assuming each prover already has a share of the SNARK witness, is
\sim
2x the prover time in the corresponding single-prover zkSNARK. Thus, we implemented zkSNARKs for
\rho_{\mathsf{SM}\text{-}\mathsf{Acc}}
and
\rho_{\mathsf{RSM}\text{-}\mathsf{Acc}}
via Merkle accumulators (see Section
1.2.4
). We used the ZoKrates toolchain
(
Eberhardt and Tai 2018
)
and the Groth16 proof system
(
Groth 2016
)
. We used the Baby Jubjub curve
(
WhiteHat et al. 2020
)
, which has similar order as BN254 and allows efficient computation of Merkle hashes for commitments for
\rho_{\mathsf{RSM}\text{-}\mathsf{Acc}}
. For creating Merkle hashes, we used the Poseidon hash function
(
Grassi et al. 2021
)
.

Figure
10
(top) also shows the per-prover times in a collaborative zkSNARK approach estimated as above (averaged over 3 runs with deviation <1.1%). We find the prover time for one
\rho_{\mathsf{SM}\text{-}\mathsf{Acc}}
or
\rho_{\mathsf{RSM}\text{-}\mathsf{Acc}}
proof against a set of size 10000 is
\sim
2.15 s, which when scaled to
10000
commitments takes
\sim
21500 s. From this, the per-prover time in collaborative zkSNARKs is estimated to be
\sim
43000 s, as shown in the column for
n=10^{4}
(for any
m
). This estimate is conservative as it does not count the time taken to securely distribute shares of the SNARK witness among the collaborating provers. This makes our
{\mathsf{DB}\text{-}\mathsf{SM}}
and
{\mathsf{DB}\text{-}\mathsf{RSM}}
proofs
\sim
86x and
\sim
18x faster than collaborative zkSNARKs. We note that our verification times (200 s and 620 s for
n=10000
) are slower than zkSNARKs’
\sim
50 s, but the dominant prover times in zkSNARKs imply that our techniques still bring drastic overall improvements.

We also ran Benarroch et al.’s
(
Benarroch et al. 2021
)
official implementation
(
Gurkan 2021
)
, which proves
\rho_{\mathsf{SM}\text{-}\mathsf{Acc}}
for a single prover (but not
\rho_{\mathsf{RSM}\text{-}\mathsf{Acc}}
). This takes
\sim
2200 s for
10000

\rho_{\mathsf{SM}\text{-}\mathsf{Acc}}
proofs, excluding the
O(n^{2})
time taken to generate the RSA accumulator witnesses (see Section
1.2.4
).

7.
Conclusion

We introduced and formalised the notion of traceable mixnets, extending traditional mixnets to provably answer useful subset queries in zero knowledge. We also proposed a traceable mixnet construction using novel distributed ZKPs of set membership and reverse set membership, which are useful in other settings too. We implemented these ZKPs and showed that they are significantly faster than the state-of-the-art techniques. Nevertheless, our current implementation is practical only for offline batch processing such as recovery in elections. Constructing traceable mixnets for real-time privacy applications is a challenging open problem.

Our gratitude also extends to the anonymous reviewers whose suggestions significantly improved the manuscript’s presentation.
