Appendix B
From honest-but-curious to malicious model

In this section, we mention steps required to derive secrecy (Definition
4
) in the general case when the adversary allows the corrupted parties (all-but-two senders, all-but-one mix-servers and the querier) to deviate from the protocol:

-

Each sender
S_{i}
must attach NIZK proofs of knowledge
({\boldsymbol{\rho}}_{\epsilon_{i}}
,
{\boldsymbol{\rho}}_{\epsilon_{r_{i}}}
,
({\boldsymbol{\rho}}^{(k)}_{\mathsf{ev}_{i}},{\boldsymbol{\rho}}^{(k)}_{\mathsf{er}_{i}})_{k\in[m]})
of plaintexts encrypted by encryptions
({\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}_{i}
,
{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}_{r_{i}}
,
({\boldsymbol{\mathsf{ev}}}^{(k)}_{i},{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{er}}}^{(k)}_{i})_{k\in[m]})
sent by it. Each
(\mathcal{M}_{k})_{k\in[m]}
must verify these proofs before processing anything.

-

For encryptions
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}^{\prime}
in
{\mathsf{DB}\text{-}\mathsf{SM}}
and
\boldsymbol{\epsilon_{S}},\boldsymbol{\epsilon_{c}},\boldsymbol{\epsilon_{\hat{r}}}
in
{\mathsf{DB}\text{-}\mathsf{RSM}}
, the querier must publish their randomnesses and each
(\mathcal{M}_{k})_{k\in[m]}
must verify that they were created correctly. Each
(\mathcal{M}_{k})_{k\in[m]}
must also verify that
\mathcal{Q}
gave valid signatures/quasi-signatures for each element in the requested set and invalid ones for its complement. Note that this also involves verifying that
\boldsymbol{c},\boldsymbol{\hat{r}}
in
{\mathsf{DB}\text{-}\mathsf{RSM}}
contain only elements in the range
[0,q]
.

-

Each
(\mathcal{M}_{k})_{k\in[m]}
must provide proofs of correct shuffle in all the shuffle protocols. In a proof of shuffle,
\mathcal{M}_{k}
for a given input ciphertext list
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}
and output ciphertext list
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}^{\prime}
proves that
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}^{\prime}
is a permutation and re-encryption of
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}
under a permutation that is consistent across
\mathsf{Mix}
,
{\mathsf{DB}\text{-}\mathsf{SM}}
and
{\mathsf{DB}\text{-}\mathsf{RSM}}
protocols (i.e., the permutation used during
{\mathsf{DB}\text{-}\mathsf{RSM}}
is the same as that used during
\mathsf{Mix}
and the permutation used during
{\mathsf{DB}\text{-}\mathsf{SM}}
is the inverse of it). Such proofs can be given efficiently using the permutation-commitment based techniques of
(
Wikström 2009
;
Terelius and Wikström 2010
)
. Each
(\mathcal{M}_{k})_{k\in[m]}
must verify proofs given by other mix-servers before participating in the corresponding threshold decryption protocols.

-

Each
(\mathcal{M}_{k})_{k\in[m]}
must provide proofs of knowledge of the blinding factors of homomorphically blinded signatures. During
{\mathsf{DB}\text{-}\mathsf{SM}}
, this involves giving proofs
{\boldsymbol{\rho}}^{(k)}_{\tilde{\sigma}_{i}}
for each
{\boldsymbol{\tilde{\epsilon}}}^{(k)}_{{\sigma}_{i}}
published by
\mathcal{M}_{k}
as a blinding of
{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}_{{\sigma}_{i}}
with blinding factor
{\boldsymbol{b}}^{(k)}_{i}
. Note that
{\boldsymbol{\rho}}^{(k)}_{\tilde{\sigma}_{i}}:=\mathsf{NIZKPK}\{(r,{\boldsymbol{b}}^{(k)}_{i}):\tilde{c}_{0}=g_{1}^{r}c_{0}^{{\boldsymbol{b}}^{(k)}_{i}}\wedge\tilde{c}_{1}=\mathsf{pk}_{\mathsf{EG}}^{r}c_{1}^{{\boldsymbol{b}}^{(k)}_{i}}\}
, where
{\boldsymbol{\epsilon_{\sigma}}}_{i}
and
{\boldsymbol{\tilde{\epsilon}_{\sigma}}}^{(k)}_{i}
are parsed as ElGamal ciphertexts
(c_{0},c_{1})
and
(\tilde{c}_{0},\tilde{c}_{1})
respectively. During
{\mathsf{DB}\text{-}\mathsf{RSM}}
, this involves giving NIZK proofs
({\boldsymbol{\rho}}^{(k)}_{bS_{j}},

{\boldsymbol{\rho}}^{(k)}_{bc_{j}},

{\boldsymbol{\rho}}^{(k)}_{br_{j}})
for each
({\boldsymbol{\epsilon_{bS}}}^{(k)}_{j},

{\boldsymbol{\epsilon_{bc}}}^{(k)}_{j},

{\boldsymbol{\epsilon_{br}}}^{(k)}_{j})
, which are encryptions of
g_{1}^{{\boldsymbol{b_{S}}}^{(k)}_{j}}
under
\mathsf{E}_{\mathsf{EG}}^{\mathsf{th}}
and of
{\boldsymbol{b_{c}^{\prime}}}^{(k)}_{j}:={\boldsymbol{b_{c}}}^{(k)}_{j}+q{\boldsymbol{\chi_{c}}}^{(k)}_{j}
and
{\boldsymbol{b_{r}^{\prime}}}^{(k)}_{j}:={\boldsymbol{b_{r}}}^{(k)}_{j}+q{\boldsymbol{\chi_{r}}}^{(k)}_{j}
under
\mathsf{E}_{\mathsf{Pa}}^{\mathsf{th}}
respectively. Here,
{\boldsymbol{\rho}}^{(k)}_{bS_{j}}:=\mathsf{NIZKPK}\{(r,{\boldsymbol{b_{S}}}^{(k)}_{j}):\tilde{c}_{0}=g_{1}^{r}\wedge\tilde{c}_{1}=g_{1}^{{\boldsymbol{b_{S}}}^{(k)}_{j}}\mathsf{pk}_{\mathsf{EG}}^{r}\}
, where
{\boldsymbol{\epsilon_{bS}}}^{(k)}_{j}
is parsed as the ElGamal ciphertext
(\tilde{c}_{0},\tilde{c}_{1})
and
{\boldsymbol{\rho}}^{(k)}_{bc_{j}}
,
{\boldsymbol{\rho}}^{(k)}_{br_{j}}
are proofs of knowledge of the plaintexts encrypted by
{\boldsymbol{\epsilon_{bc}}}^{(k)}_{j}
,
{\boldsymbol{\epsilon_{br}}}^{(k)}_{j}
respectively.

-

During the
\mathsf{E}_{\mathsf{EG}}^{\mathsf{th}}.\mathsf{TDec}
and
\mathsf{E}_{\mathsf{Pa}}^{\mathsf{th}}.\mathsf{TDec}
protocols, each
(\mathcal{M}_{k})_{k\in[m]}
must provide proofs that they produced correct decryption shares. Each
(\mathcal{M}_{k})_{k\in[m]}
should proceed with stage 2 only if these proofs pass.

-

During the
{\mathsf{DB}\text{-}\mathsf{RSM}}
protocol, each
(\mathcal{M}_{k})_{k\in[m]}
must provide a proof of knowledge of the opening of
{\mathfrak{z}_{1}}^{(k^{*})}
. Each
(\mathcal{M}_{k})_{k\in[m]}
should proceed only if the proof passes.

Note that the DPKs in
{\mathsf{DB}\text{-}\mathsf{SM}}
and
{\mathsf{DB}\text{-}\mathsf{RSM}}
already employ the Fiat-Shamir heuristic
(
Fiat and Shamir 1986
)
which makes them general ZKPs in the random oracle model (see Section
3.1.5
).

In Section
C.5
, we sketch a proof that our construction with the above steps protects secrecy (Definition
4
) against general malicious adversaries.

Appendix C
Proofs

C.1.
Proof for Theorem
1

It can be inspected that when all the parties are honest, inputs to protocols
{\mathsf{DB}\text{-}\mathsf{SM}}
and
{\mathsf{DB}\text{-}\mathsf{RSM}}
satisfy the preconditions mentioned in Figures
8
and
9
, respectively. This is also true for the reruns of
{\mathsf{DB}\text{-}\mathsf{SM}}
and
{\mathsf{DB}\text{-}\mathsf{RSM}}
in the
\mathsf{BTraceIn}/\mathsf{BTraceOut}
calls against the complement sets. Thus, by Lemma
1
,
I^{*}
and
I^{*}_{\mathsf{c}}
obtained by
\mathcal{Q}
in a
\mathsf{BTraceIn}
call satisfy
I^{*}=\{i\in I\mid{\boldsymbol{v}}_{i}\in{\boldsymbol{v}}_{J}^{\prime}\}
and
I^{*}_{\mathsf{c}}=\{i\in I\mid{\boldsymbol{v}}_{i}\in{\boldsymbol{v}}_{[n]\setminus J}^{\prime}\}
. Also, the correctness of
\mathsf{Mix}
implies that for each
i\in I\subseteq[n]
,
{\boldsymbol{v}}_{i}\in\{{\boldsymbol{v}}_{j}^{\prime}\mid j\in[n]\}
, i.e.,
{\boldsymbol{v}}_{i}\in{\boldsymbol{v}}_{J}^{\prime}\cup{\boldsymbol{v}}_{[n]\setminus J}^{\prime}
for any
J\subseteq[n]
. Thus,
I^{*}\cup I^{*}_{\mathsf{c}}=I
, which implies that
\mathcal{Q}
does not abort and outputs a
{\boldsymbol{c}}_{I^{*}}
that satisfies the first condition of
\mathsf{Exp}_{\mathsf{completeness}}
(Figure
3
). By a similar argument using Lemma
2
, it follows that
\mathcal{Q}
does not abort in a
\mathsf{BTraceOut}
call and outputs a
{\boldsymbol{v}}_{J^{*}}^{\prime}
that satisfies the second condition of
\mathsf{Exp}_{\mathsf{completeness}}
.

Lemma 0.

If inputs
(\mathsf{pk}_{\mathsf{EG}},

\boldsymbol{\gamma},

\boldsymbol{v}^{\prime},

I,

J,

(\mathcal{M}_{k}[\![{\mathsf{sk}}^{(k)}_{\mathsf{EG}},

{\pi}^{(k)},

{\boldsymbol{v}}^{(k)},

{\boldsymbol{r}}^{(k)}]\!])_{k\in[m]})
of a
{\mathsf{DB}\text{-}\mathsf{SM}}
invocation satisfy the preconditions mentioned in Figure
8
and all the parties are honest then
\mathcal{Q}
outputs
I^{*}=\{i\in I\mid\exists j\in J:\sum_{k\in[m]}{\boldsymbol{v}}^{(k)}_{i}={\boldsymbol{v}}_{j}^{\prime}\}
.

Proof.

Note that for honest mix-servers, the
i^{\text{th}}
DPK passes iff
\mathcal{M}_{k}
uses
({\boldsymbol{v}}^{(k)}_{i},{\boldsymbol{r}}^{(k)}_{i},{\boldsymbol{b}}^{(k)}_{i})
such that
({\boldsymbol{v}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{r}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{b}}_{i}):=(\sum_{k\in[m]}{\boldsymbol{v}}^{(k)}_{i},

\sum_{k\in[m]}{\boldsymbol{r}}^{(k)}_{i},

\sum_{k\in[m]}{\boldsymbol{b}}^{(k)}_{i})
satisfy the predicate
p_{\mathsf{BB}_{i}}
. The equation
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{i}=g_{1}^{{\boldsymbol{v}}_{i}}h_{1}^{{\boldsymbol{r}}_{i}}
of
p_{\mathsf{BB}_{i}}
is trivially satisfied by the correctness of
({\boldsymbol{v}}^{(k)}_{i},{\boldsymbol{r}}^{(k)}_{i})
. Next, note that
{\boldsymbol{\tilde{\sigma}}}_{i}=({\boldsymbol{\sigma^{\prime}}}_{\pi^{-1}(i)})^{{\sum_{k\in[m]}{\boldsymbol{b}}^{(k)}_{i}}}
, by the homomorphism of
\mathsf{E}_{\mathsf{EG}}^{\mathsf{th}}
. Let
j\in[n]
be the index to which index
i
is mapped after permutation, i.e.,
i=\pi(j)
or equivalently
\pi^{-1}(i)=j
. Correctness of input conditions implies
{\boldsymbol{v}}_{j}^{\prime}=\sum_{k\in[m]}{\boldsymbol{v}}^{(k)}_{\pi(j)}=\sum_{k\in[m]}{\boldsymbol{v}}^{(k)}_{i}
. Thus,
{\boldsymbol{\tilde{\sigma}}}_{i}=({\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{j}^{\prime})^{\sum_{k\in[m]}{\boldsymbol{b}}^{(k)}_{i}}
, which equals
g_{1}^{\frac{\sum_{k\in[m]}{\boldsymbol{b}}^{(k)}_{i}}{x+{\boldsymbol{v}}_{j}^{\prime}}}

=

g_{1}^{\frac{\sum_{k\in[m]}{\boldsymbol{b}}^{(k)}_{i}}{x+\sum_{k\in[m]}{\boldsymbol{v}}^{(k)}_{i}}}
if
j\in J
and
g_{1}^{\sum_{k\in[m]}{\boldsymbol{b}}^{(k)}_{i}}
if
j\not\in J
. In the first case, the second equation of
p_{\mathsf{BB}_{i}}
passes; in the second case, it fails. Since the DPK is run only for
i\in I
,
I^{*}
is exactly as claimed.
∎

Lemma 0.

If inputs
(\mathsf{pk}_{\mathsf{EG}},

\mathsf{pk}_{\mathsf{Pa}},

\boldsymbol{\gamma},

\boldsymbol{\rho_{\gamma}},

\boldsymbol{\epsilon_{r}},

\boldsymbol{v}^{\prime},

I,

J,

(\mathcal{M}_{k}[\![{\mathsf{sk}}^{(k)}_{\mathsf{EG}},

{\mathsf{sk}}^{(k)}_{\mathsf{Pa}},{\pi}^{(k)},{\boldsymbol{v}}^{(k)},

{\boldsymbol{r}}^{(k)}]\!])_{k\in[m]})
of a
{\mathsf{DB}\text{-}\mathsf{RSM}}
invocation satisfy the preconditions mentioned in Figure
9
and all the parties are honest then
\mathcal{Q}
outputs
J^{*}=\{j\in J\mid\exists i\in I:\sum_{k\in[m]}{\boldsymbol{v}}^{(k)}_{i}={\boldsymbol{v}}_{j}^{\prime}\}
.

Proof.

As in Lemma
1
, let
i
be s.t.
i=\pi(j)
. By correctness of inputs,
{\boldsymbol{v}}_{j}^{\prime}=\sum_{k\in[m]}{\boldsymbol{v}}^{(k)}_{i}
,
{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}_{r_{i}}\leftarrow\mathsf{E}_{\mathsf{Pa}}^{\mathsf{th}}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{pk}_{\mathsf{Pa}},\sum_{k\in[m]}{\boldsymbol{r}}^{(k)}_{i})
. Then, it can be inspected that the following equalities hold:

-

{\boldsymbol{\tilde{S}}}_{j}^{\prime}

=

(f_{1}g_{1}^{\sum_{k\in[m]}{\boldsymbol{v}}^{(k)}_{i}}

h_{1}^{{\boldsymbol{\hat{r}}}_{i}+\sum_{k\in[m]}{\boldsymbol{r}}^{(k)}_{i}})^{\frac{1}{x+{\boldsymbol{c}}_{i}}}

g_{1}^{\sum_{k\in[m]}{\boldsymbol{b_{S}}}^{(k)}_{j}}
if
i\in I
else
f_{1}^{0}
,

-

{\boldsymbol{\tilde{c}}}_{j}^{\prime}={\boldsymbol{c}}_{i}+\sum_{k\in[m]}{\boldsymbol{b_{c}}}^{(k)}_{j}
,

-

{\boldsymbol{\tilde{r}}}_{j}^{\prime}={\boldsymbol{\hat{r}}}_{i}+\sum_{k\in[m]}{\boldsymbol{r}}^{(k)}_{i}+\sum_{k\in[m]}{\boldsymbol{b_{r}}}^{(k)}_{j}

Therefore,
({\boldsymbol{\tilde{S}}}_{j}^{\prime}g_{1}^{-{\boldsymbol{b}}_{{\boldsymbol{S}}_{j}}},{\boldsymbol{\tilde{c}}}_{j}^{\prime}-{\boldsymbol{b}}_{{\boldsymbol{c}}_{j}},{\boldsymbol{\tilde{r}}}_{j}^{\prime}-{\boldsymbol{b}}_{{\boldsymbol{r}}_{j}})
satisfies the BBS+ verification equation
e({\boldsymbol{\tilde{S}}}_{j}^{\prime}g_{1}^{-{\boldsymbol{b}}_{{\boldsymbol{S}}_{j}}},yf_{2}^{{\boldsymbol{\tilde{c}}}_{j}^{\prime}-{\boldsymbol{b}}_{{\boldsymbol{c}}_{j}}})=e(f_{1}g_{1}^{{\boldsymbol{v}}_{j}^{\prime}}h_{1}^{{\boldsymbol{\tilde{r}}}_{j}^{\prime}-{\boldsymbol{b}}_{{\boldsymbol{r}}_{j}}},f_{2})
if
i\in I
, where
({\boldsymbol{b_{S}}}_{j},{\boldsymbol{b_{c}}}_{j},{\boldsymbol{b_{r}}}_{j})

:=

(\sum_{k\in[m]}{\boldsymbol{b_{S}}}^{(k)}_{j},

\sum_{k\in[m]}{\boldsymbol{b_{c}}}^{(k)}_{j},

\sum_{k\in[m]}{\boldsymbol{b_{r}}}^{(k)}_{j})
. So:

(4)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\quad\quad e({\boldsymbol{\tilde{S}}}_{j}^{\prime}g_{1}^{-{\boldsymbol{b_{S}}}_{j}},yf_{2}^{{\boldsymbol{\tilde{c}}}_{j}^{\prime}-{\boldsymbol{b_{c}}}_{j}})=e(f_{1}g_{1}^{{\boldsymbol{v}}_{j}^{\prime}}h_{1}^{{\boldsymbol{\tilde{r}}}_{j}^{\prime}-{\boldsymbol{b_{r}}}_{j}},f_{2})\\
&\Leftrightarrow\frac{e({\boldsymbol{\tilde{S}}}_{j}^{\prime},yf_{2}^{{\boldsymbol{\tilde{c}}}_{j}^{\prime}-{\boldsymbol{b_{c}}}_{j}})}{e(g_{1}^{{\boldsymbol{b_{S}}}_{j}},yf_{2}^{{\boldsymbol{\tilde{c}}}_{j}^{\prime}-{\boldsymbol{b_{c}}}_{j}})}=e(f_{1}g_{1}^{{\boldsymbol{v}}_{j}^{\prime}}h_{1}^{{\boldsymbol{\tilde{r}}}_{j}^{\prime}-{\boldsymbol{b_{r}}}_{j}},f_{2})\\
&\Leftrightarrow\frac{e({\boldsymbol{\tilde{S}}}_{j}^{\prime},yf_{2}^{{\boldsymbol{\tilde{c}}}_{j}^{\prime}})}{e(f_{1}g_{1}^{{\boldsymbol{v}}_{j}^{\prime}}h_{1}^{{\boldsymbol{\tilde{r}}}_{j}^{\prime}},f_{2})}=\\
&\quad\quad e({\boldsymbol{\tilde{S}}}_{j}^{\prime},f_{2})^{{\boldsymbol{b_{c}}}_{j}}e(g_{1},yf_{2}^{{\boldsymbol{\tilde{c}}}_{j}^{\prime}})^{{\boldsymbol{b_{S}}}_{j}}e(h_{1},f_{2})^{-{\boldsymbol{b_{r}}}_{j}}e(g_{1},f_{2})^{-{\boldsymbol{b_{S}}}_{j}{\boldsymbol{b_{c}}}_{j}}\\
&\Leftrightarrow\mathfrak{z}_{2}=\mathfrak{g}_{1}^{{\boldsymbol{b_{c}}}_{j}}\mathfrak{g}_{2}^{{\boldsymbol{b_{S}}}_{j}}\mathfrak{h}_{1}^{{\boldsymbol{b_{r}}}_{j}}\mathfrak{h}_{2}^{{\boldsymbol{b_{S}}}_{j}{\boldsymbol{b_{c}}}_{j}}\end{split}

Thus, with the mix-servers holding shares of
({\boldsymbol{b_{S}}}_{j},{\boldsymbol{b_{c}}}_{j},{\boldsymbol{b_{r}}}_{j})
and
\delta_{1}:={\boldsymbol{b_{S}}}_{j}{\boldsymbol{b_{c}}}_{j}
and
\delta_{2}:=\delta_{0}\delta_{1}
, all equations of the predicate
p_{\mathsf{BBS}+_{j}}
of the DPK are satisfied if
i\in I
. If
i\not\in I
, the last equation of
p_{\mathsf{BBS}+_{j}}
is not satisfied. The claim follows.
∎

C.2.
Proof for Theorem
2

Suppose for contradiction that there is a PPT adversary
\mathcal{A}
such that
\mathsf{Exp}_{\mathsf{soundness}}
(Figure
4
) outputs
1
with non-negligible probability. Note first that
\mathcal{Q}
always outputs
I^{*}\subseteq I
in
{\mathsf{DB}\text{-}\mathsf{SM}}
and
J^{*}\subseteq J
in
{\mathsf{DB}\text{-}\mathsf{RSM}}
. Thus,
{\boldsymbol{c}}_{I^{*}}\subseteq{\boldsymbol{c}}_{I}
and
{\boldsymbol{v}}_{J^{*}}^{\prime}\subseteq{\boldsymbol{v}}_{J}^{\prime}
. We now consider the following cases:

Case 1:
{\boldsymbol{c}}_{I^{*}}\neq\{{\boldsymbol{c}}_{i}\in{\boldsymbol{c}}_{I}\mid{\boldsymbol{v}}_{i}\in{\boldsymbol{v}}_{J}^{\prime}\}.
This leads to the following sub-cases:

-

Case 1.1:
\exists{\boldsymbol{c}}_{i}\in{\boldsymbol{c}}_{I^{*}}:{\boldsymbol{v}}_{i}\not\in{\boldsymbol{v}}_{J}^{\prime}.
As per Figure
7
,
{\boldsymbol{c}}_{i}
contains
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{i}=g_{1}^{{\boldsymbol{v}}_{i}}h_{1}^{{\boldsymbol{r}}_{i}}
for some
{\boldsymbol{r}}_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}
. Since
{\boldsymbol{c}}_{i}\in{\boldsymbol{c}}_{I^{*}}
,
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{i}\in{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{I^{*}}
. Thus, by Lemma
3
, a PPT extractor can extract a tuple
(j^{*},r^{*})
such that
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{i}=g_{1}^{{\boldsymbol{v}}_{j^{*}}^{\prime}}h_{1}^{r^{*}}
and
{\boldsymbol{v}}_{j^{*}}^{\prime}\in{\boldsymbol{v}}_{J}^{\prime}
. The requirement
{\boldsymbol{v}}_{i}\not\in{\boldsymbol{v}}_{J}^{\prime}
thus implies that
{\boldsymbol{v}}_{i}\neq{\boldsymbol{v}}_{j^{*}}^{\prime}
. This allows producing two different openings
({\boldsymbol{v}}_{j^{*}}^{\prime},r^{*})
and
({\boldsymbol{v}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{r}}_{i})
for Pedersen commitment
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{i}
, which is a contradiction under the discrete logarithm assumption in
\mathbb{G}_{1}
.

-

Case 1.2:
\exists{\boldsymbol{c}}_{i}\in{\boldsymbol{c}}_{I\setminus I^{*}}:{\boldsymbol{v}}_{i}\in{\boldsymbol{v}}_{J}^{\prime}.
Note that since
\mathcal{Q}
produces a
{\boldsymbol{c}}_{I^{*}}
and does not abort, it must be that
I^{*}\cup I^{*}_{\mathsf{c}}=I
during the
\mathsf{BTraceIn}
call. Thus,
I^{*}_{\mathsf{c}}=I\setminus I^{*}
. Further, since all
{\boldsymbol{v}}_{i}^{\prime}
s are distinct,
{\boldsymbol{v}}_{i}\in{\boldsymbol{v}}_{J}^{\prime}\implies{\boldsymbol{v}}_{i}\not\in{\boldsymbol{v}}_{[n]\setminus J}^{\prime}
. Thus, this case can be restated as follows:
\exists{\boldsymbol{c}}_{i}\in{\boldsymbol{c}}_{I^{*}_{\mathsf{c}}}:{\boldsymbol{v}}_{i}\not\in{\boldsymbol{v}}_{[n]\setminus J}^{\prime}
. Thus, by applying Lemma
3
for the second
{\mathsf{DB}\text{-}\mathsf{SM}}
call in
\mathsf{BTraceIn}
and proceeding as the previous case, we conclude that this case is not possible.

Case 2:
{\boldsymbol{v}}_{J^{*}}^{\prime}\neq\{{\boldsymbol{v}}_{j}^{\prime}\in{\boldsymbol{v}}_{J}^{\prime}\mid{\boldsymbol{v}}_{j}^{\prime}\in{\boldsymbol{v}}_{I}\}.
This leads to the following sub-cases:

-

Case 2.1:
\exists{\boldsymbol{v}}_{j}^{\prime}\in{\boldsymbol{v}}_{J^{*}}^{\prime}:{\boldsymbol{v}}_{j}^{\prime}\not\in{\boldsymbol{v}}_{I}.
Since
{\boldsymbol{v}}_{j}^{\prime}\in{\boldsymbol{v}}_{J^{*}}^{\prime}
, by Lemma
4
, a PPT extractor can extract a tuple
(i^{*},r^{*})
such that
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{i^{*}}=g_{1}^{{\boldsymbol{v}}_{j}^{\prime}}h_{1}^{r^{*}}
and
i^{*}\in I
. Since
i^{*}\in I
, the requirement
{\boldsymbol{v}}_{j}^{\prime}\not\in{\boldsymbol{v}}_{I}
implies
{\boldsymbol{v}}_{j}^{\prime}\neq{\boldsymbol{v}}_{i^{*}}
. As per Figure
7
,
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{i^{*}}\leftarrow g_{1}^{{\boldsymbol{v}}_{i^{*}}}h_{1}^{{\boldsymbol{r}}_{i^{*}}}
for some
{\boldsymbol{r}}_{i^{*}}\in\mathbb{Z}_{q}
. This allows producing two different openings
({\boldsymbol{v}}_{j}^{\prime},r^{*})
and
({\boldsymbol{v}}_{i^{*}},{\boldsymbol{r}}_{i^{*}})
for
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{i^{*}}
, which leads to a contradiction.

-

Case 2.2:
\exists{\boldsymbol{v}}_{j}^{\prime}\in{\boldsymbol{v}}_{J\setminus J^{*}}^{\prime}:{\boldsymbol{v}}_{j}^{\prime}\in{\boldsymbol{v}}_{I}.
Note that since
\mathcal{Q}
produces a
{\boldsymbol{v}}_{J^{*}}^{\prime}
and does not abort, it must be that
J^{*}\cup J^{*}_{\mathsf{c}}=J
during the
\mathsf{BTraceIn}
call. Thus,
J^{*}_{\mathsf{c}}=J\setminus J^{*}
. Further, since all
{\boldsymbol{v}}_{i}
s are distinct,
{\boldsymbol{v}}_{j}^{\prime}\in{\boldsymbol{v}}_{I}\implies{\boldsymbol{v}}_{j}^{\prime}\not\in{\boldsymbol{v}}_{[n]\setminus I}
. Thus, this case can be restated as follows:
\exists{\boldsymbol{v}}_{j}^{\prime}\in{\boldsymbol{v}}_{J^{*}_{\mathsf{c}}}:{\boldsymbol{v}}_{j}^{\prime}\not\in{\boldsymbol{v}}_{[n]\setminus I}
. Thus, by applying Lemma
4
for the second
{\mathsf{DB}\text{-}\mathsf{RSM}}
call in
\mathsf{BTraceOut}
and proceeding as the previous case, we conclude that this case is not possible.

Lemma 0.

If
\mathcal{Q}
participates in the
{\mathsf{DB}\text{-}\mathsf{SM}}
protocol with common input
(\mathsf{pk}_{\mathsf{EG}},\boldsymbol{\gamma},\boldsymbol{v}^{\prime},I,J)
and outputs
I^{*}
, then for all PPT adversaries
\mathcal{A}
controlling
(\mathcal{M}_{k})_{k\in[m]}
and for all
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{i}\in{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{I^{*}}
, there exists a PPT extractor
\mathcal{E}
that outputs a
(j^{*},r^{*})
such that
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{i}=g_{1}^{{\boldsymbol{v}}_{j^{*}}^{\prime}}h_{1}^{r^{*}}\wedge{\boldsymbol{v}}_{j^{*}}^{\prime}\in{\boldsymbol{v}}_{J}^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Note that the verification steps of
\mathcal{Q}
in
{\mathsf{DB}\text{-}\mathsf{SM}}
for a given
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{i}\in{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{I}
are exactly those of the verifier of the single-prover ZKP of set membership
(
Camenisch et al. 2008
)
for commitment
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{i}
against set
\phi:={\boldsymbol{v}}_{J}^{\prime}
when extended to our asymmetric pairing setting. Thus, for each
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{i}\in{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{I^{*}}
, by the special soundness of the single-prover ZKP
(
Camenisch et al. 2008
)
under the
n
-Strong Diffie-Hellman assumption in
(\mathbb{G}_{1},\mathbb{G}_{2})
, a PPT extractor
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
can extract
v^{*},r^{*}
such that
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{i}=g_{1}^{v^{*}}h_{1}^{r^{*}}\wedge v^{*}\in\phi
.
\mathcal{E}
simply runs
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
, finds
j^{*}
such that
v^{*}={\boldsymbol{v}}_{j^{*}}^{\prime}
and outputs
(j^{*},r^{*})
.
∎

Lemma 0.

If
\mathcal{Q}
participates in the
{\mathsf{DB}\text{-}\mathsf{RSM}}
protocol with common input
(\mathsf{pk}_{\mathsf{EG}},\mathsf{pk}_{\mathsf{Pa}},\boldsymbol{\gamma},\boldsymbol{\rho_{\gamma}},\boldsymbol{\epsilon_{r}},\boldsymbol{v}^{\prime},I,J)
and outputs
J^{*}
, then for all PPT adversaries
\mathcal{A}
controlling
(\mathcal{M}_{k})_{k\in[m]}
and for all
{\boldsymbol{v}}_{j}^{\prime}\in{\boldsymbol{v}}_{J^{*}}^{\prime}
, there exists a PPT extractor
\mathcal{E}
that outputs an
(i^{*},r^{*})
such that
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{i^{*}}=g_{1}^{{\boldsymbol{v}}_{j}^{\prime}}h_{1}^{r^{*}}\wedge i^{*}\in I
.

Proof.

1

\mathcal{E}(\mathsf{pk}_{\mathsf{EG}},\mathsf{pk}_{\mathsf{Pa}},\boldsymbol{\gamma},\boldsymbol{\rho_{\gamma}},\boldsymbol{v}^{\prime},I,J)
:

2

\mathcal{E}
:

for each
i\in I
:

({\boldsymbol{v}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{r}}_{i})\leftarrow\mathcal{E}_{1}({\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{\rho_{\gamma}}}_{i})

3

\mathcal{E}\rightarrow\mathcal{C}
:

({\boldsymbol{v}}_{i})_{i\in I}
(BBS+ signature queries)

4

\mathcal{C}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
:

y,({\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{i})_{i\in I}

5

\mathcal{E}
:

for each
i\in[n]
:

6

if
i\in I
:

({\boldsymbol{S}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{c}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{r}}}_{i}):={\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{i}
;
{\boldsymbol{\hat{r}}}_{i}\leftarrow{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{r}}}_{i}-{\boldsymbol{r}}_{i}

7

else:

{\boldsymbol{S}}_{i}\leftarrow f_{1}^{0}
;
{\boldsymbol{c}}_{i}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}
;
{\boldsymbol{\hat{r}}}_{i}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}

8

endfor

9

(\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{S},\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{c},\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{\hat{r}})\leftarrow(\mathsf{E}_{\mathsf{EG}}^{\mathsf{th}}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{pk}_{\mathsf{EG}},\boldsymbol{S}),\mathsf{E}_{\mathsf{Pa}}^{\mathsf{th}}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{pk}_{\mathsf{Pa}},\boldsymbol{c}),

\mathsf{E}_{\mathsf{Pa}}^{\mathsf{th}}.\mathsf{Enc}(\mathsf{pk}_{\mathsf{Pa}},\boldsymbol{\hat{r}}))

10

\mathcal{E}\rightarrow\mathcal{A}
:

y,\boldsymbol{\hat{\sigma}}:=(\boldsymbol{S},\boldsymbol{c},\boldsymbol{\hat{r}}),\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{\hat{\sigma}}:=(\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{S},\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{c},\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{\hat{r}})

11

\mathcal{A}\rightarrow\mathcal{E}
:

\boldsymbol{\tilde{\sigma}}^{\prime}:=(\boldsymbol{\tilde{S}}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{\tilde{c}}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{\tilde{r}}^{\prime})

12

\mathcal{E}
:

for each
j\in J
:

13

Compute
\mathfrak{g}_{1},\mathfrak{g}_{2},\mathfrak{h}_{1},\mathfrak{h}_{2},\mathfrak{h}_{3},\mathfrak{z}_{1},\mathfrak{z}_{2}
from
{\boldsymbol{\tilde{\sigma}}}_{j}^{\prime}:=({\boldsymbol{\tilde{S}}}_{j}^{\prime},{\boldsymbol{\tilde{c}}}_{j}^{\prime},{\boldsymbol{\tilde{r}}}_{j}^{\prime})

14

({\boldsymbol{b_{S}}}_{j},{\boldsymbol{b_{c}}}_{j},{\boldsymbol{b_{r}}}_{j},\delta_{0},\delta_{1},\delta_{2})\leftarrow

\mathcal{E}_{2}(\mathfrak{g}_{1},\mathfrak{g}_{2},\mathfrak{h}_{1},\mathfrak{h}_{2},\mathfrak{h}_{3},\mathfrak{z}_{1},\mathfrak{z}_{2})

15

{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{j}^{\prime}:=({\boldsymbol{\tilde{S}}}_{j}^{\prime}g_{1}^{-{\boldsymbol{b_{S}}}_{j}},{\boldsymbol{\tilde{c}}}_{j}^{\prime}-{\boldsymbol{b_{c}}}_{j},{\boldsymbol{\tilde{r}}}_{j}^{\prime}-{\boldsymbol{b_{r}}}_{j})

16

for each
i\in I
:

17

if
({\boldsymbol{v}}_{i}={\boldsymbol{v}}_{j}^{\prime})
:
output
(i,{\boldsymbol{r}}_{i})

18

endfor

19

output
({\boldsymbol{v}}_{j}^{\prime},{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{j}^{\prime})

20

endfor

Figure 12
.
Extractor
\mathcal{E}
.

We construct an algorithm
\mathcal{E}
that for each
{\boldsymbol{v}}_{j}^{\prime}\in{\boldsymbol{v}}_{J^{*}}^{\prime}
either outputs a desired tuple
(i^{*},r^{*})
or forges a BBS+ signature, using extractors
\mathcal{E}_{1}
for the NIZK proofs of knowledge of commitment openings
{\boldsymbol{\rho}}_{\gamma_{i}}
and
\mathcal{E}_{2}
for the DPKs for
p_{\mathsf{BBS}+_{j}}
. On one end,
\mathcal{E}
interacts with adversary
\mathcal{A}
controlling
(\mathcal{M}_{k})_{k\in[m]}
in the
{\mathsf{DB}\text{-}\mathsf{RSM}}
protocol; on the other, with the challenger
\mathcal{C}
of the BBS+ signature unforgeability game. See Figure
12
.

For each
i\in I
,
\mathcal{E}
first extracts opening
({\boldsymbol{v}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{r}}_{i})
of commitment
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{i}
from the NIZK proof
{\boldsymbol{\rho_{\gamma}}}_{i}
using extractor
\mathcal{E}_{1}
(line 2). It then obtains BBS+ public key
y
and signatures for each
({\boldsymbol{v}}_{i})_{i\in I}
from the BBS+ challenger
\mathcal{C}
(lines 3-4), derives quasi-signatures from them using
{\boldsymbol{r}}_{i}
(line 6) and forwards the quasi-signatures and their encrypted versions to
\mathcal{A}
, along with invalid quasi-signatures for
i\not\in I
— similar to
\mathcal{Q}
(lines 5-10).
\mathcal{A}
responds with blinded permuted signatures
\boldsymbol{\tilde{\sigma}}^{\prime}
(line 11), as
(\mathcal{M}_{k})_{k\in[m]}
do in the real protocol at the end of stage 1. In stage 2, for each
j\in J
,
\mathcal{E}
extracts blinding factors for the blinded signature
{\boldsymbol{\tilde{\sigma}}}_{j}^{\prime}
using extractor
\mathcal{E}_{2}
(lines 13-14), from which it obtains an unblinded signature
{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{j}^{\prime}
(line 15).
\mathcal{E}
then attempts to find an opening
{\boldsymbol{v}}_{i}
extracted by
\mathcal{E}_{1}
for some commitment
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{i}\in{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{I}
such that
{\boldsymbol{v}}_{i}={\boldsymbol{v}}_{j}^{\prime}
, and returns the corresponding tuple
(i,{\boldsymbol{r}}_{i})
(lines 16-18). If no such
{\boldsymbol{v}}_{i}
exists, it outputs the message-signature tuple
({\boldsymbol{v}}_{j}^{\prime},{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{j}^{\prime})
(line 19).

Note that
\mathcal{Q}
produced some
J^{*}
and did not abort. In this case, the view produced by
\mathcal{E}
to
\mathcal{A}
is identical to that produced by
\mathcal{Q}
to
(\mathcal{M}_{k})_{k\in[m]}
. Further, for some
{\boldsymbol{v}}_{j}^{\prime}\in{\boldsymbol{v}}_{J^{*}}^{\prime}
, if
{\boldsymbol{v}}_{i}={\boldsymbol{v}}_{j}^{\prime}
for some
i\in I
,
(i,{\boldsymbol{r}}_{i})
output in line 17 is a desired tuple since
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{i}=g_{1}^{{\boldsymbol{v}}_{i}}h_{1}^{{\boldsymbol{r}}_{i}}=g_{1}^{{\boldsymbol{v}}_{j}^{\prime}}h_{1}^{{\boldsymbol{r}}_{i}}
and
i\in I
(the first equality follows by the soundness of the proof of knowledge of commitment openings; the second because
{\boldsymbol{v}}_{i}={\boldsymbol{v}}_{j}^{\prime}
). If no such
{\boldsymbol{v}}_{i}
exists, we show that the tuple
({\boldsymbol{v}}_{j}^{\prime},{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{j}^{\prime})
output in line 19 is a valid BBS+ signature forgery:

-

Since for all
i\in I
,
{\boldsymbol{v}}_{i}\neq{\boldsymbol{v}}_{j}^{\prime}
, a BBS+ signature for
{\boldsymbol{v}}_{j}^{\prime}
was not queried from
\mathcal{C}
in line 3.

-

Since
{\boldsymbol{v}}_{j}^{\prime}\in{\boldsymbol{v}}_{J^{*}}^{\prime}
, the DPK for
p_{\mathsf{BBS}+_{j}}
must have passed. Thus, by the soundness of DPKs:

(5)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{z}_{1}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{h}_{2}^{{\boldsymbol{b_{S}}}_{j}}\mathfrak{h}_{3}^{\delta_{0}}

(6)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{z}^{{\boldsymbol{b_{c}}}_{j}}_{1}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{h}_{2}^{\delta_{1}}\mathfrak{h}_{3}^{\delta_{2}},

(7)

\displaystyle\mathfrak{z}_{2}

\displaystyle=\mathfrak{g}_{1}^{{\boldsymbol{b_{c}}}_{j}}\mathfrak{g}_{2}^{{\boldsymbol{b_{S}}}_{j}}\mathfrak{h}_{1}^{{\boldsymbol{b_{r}}}_{j}}\mathfrak{h}_{2}^{\delta_{1}}

From Equations
5
and
6
,
\mathfrak{h}_{2}^{{\boldsymbol{b_{S}}}_{j}{\boldsymbol{b_{c}}}_{j}}\mathfrak{h}_{3}^{\delta_{0}{\boldsymbol{b_{c}}}_{j}}=\mathfrak{h}_{2}^{\delta_{1}}\mathfrak{h}_{3}^{\delta_{2}}
. It must be that
\delta_{1}={\boldsymbol{b_{S}}}_{j}{\boldsymbol{b_{c}}}_{j}
, otherwise two different openings
(\delta_{1},\delta_{2})
and
({\boldsymbol{b_{S}}}_{j}{\boldsymbol{b_{c}}}_{j},\delta_{0}{\boldsymbol{b_{c}}}_{j})
for the Pedersen commitment
\mathfrak{z}^{{\boldsymbol{b_{c}}}_{j}}_{1}
can be produced. Equation
7
thus implies
\mathfrak{z}_{2}=\mathfrak{g}_{1}^{{\boldsymbol{b_{c}}}_{j}}\mathfrak{g}_{2}^{{\boldsymbol{b_{S}}}_{j}}\mathfrak{h}_{1}^{{\boldsymbol{b_{r}}}_{j}}\mathfrak{h}_{2}^{{\boldsymbol{b_{S}}}_{j}{\boldsymbol{b_{c}}}_{j}}
, which implies that
{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{j}^{\prime}:=({\boldsymbol{\tilde{S}}}_{j}^{\prime}g_{1}^{-{\boldsymbol{b_{S}}}_{j}},{\boldsymbol{\tilde{c}}}_{j}^{\prime}-{\boldsymbol{b_{c}}}_{j},{\boldsymbol{\tilde{r}}}_{j}^{\prime}-{\boldsymbol{b_{r}}}_{j})
satisfies the BBS+ signature verification equation on message
{\boldsymbol{v}}_{j}^{\prime}
under public key
y
:
e({\boldsymbol{\tilde{S}}}_{j}^{\prime}g_{1}^{-{\boldsymbol{b_{S}}}_{j}},yf_{2}^{{\boldsymbol{\tilde{c}}}_{j}^{\prime}-{\boldsymbol{b_{c}}}_{j}})=e(f_{1}g_{1}^{{\boldsymbol{v}}_{j}^{\prime}}h_{1}^{{\boldsymbol{\tilde{r}}}_{j}^{\prime}-{\boldsymbol{b_{r}}}_{j}},f_{2})
(see Equation
4
; Lemma
2
).

Since at most
n
signature queries could have been made, forging a BBS+ signature is not possible under the
n
-Strong Diffie Hellman assumption in
(\mathbb{G}_{1},\mathbb{G}_{2})

(
Au et al. 2006
)
. Thus, for each
{\boldsymbol{v}}_{j}^{\prime}\in{\boldsymbol{v}}_{J^{*}}^{\prime}
, some
i\in I
such that
{\boldsymbol{v}}_{i}={\boldsymbol{v}}_{j}^{\prime}
must exist and a desired tuple
(i,{\boldsymbol{r}}_{i})
must have been produced.
∎

C.3.
Proof for Theorem
3

We prove the theorem by considering the following sequence of hybrid experiments (the complete hybrid experiments are shown in Figures
13
to
32
; solid boxes denote lines changed in a given hybrid and dashed boxes denote lines that change in the next hybrid):

-

E_{1}
(Figure
13
):
E_{1}
is the original secrecy game
\mathsf{Exp}_{\mathsf{secrecy}}
instantiated for our protocol.

-

E_{2}
(Figure
14
): In
E_{2}
, NIZKPKs
{\boldsymbol{\rho_{\gamma}}}_{i_{0}}
and
{\boldsymbol{\rho_{\gamma}}}_{i_{1}}
are simulated.
E_{2}
is indistinguishable from
E_{1}
in the random oracle model because of the ZK property of the NIZK proof.

-

E_{3}
(Figure
15
): In
E_{3}
, shares of
{\boldsymbol{v}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{r}}_{i}
for
i\in\{i_{0},i_{1}\}
are drawn as
({\boldsymbol{v}}^{(k)}_{i},{\boldsymbol{r}}^{(k)}_{i})_{k\neq k^{*}}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q}
and
{\boldsymbol{v}}^{(k^{*})}_{i}\leftarrow{\boldsymbol{v}}_{i}-\sum_{k\neq k^{*}}{\boldsymbol{v}}^{(k)}_{i}
,
{\boldsymbol{r}}^{(k^{*})}_{i}\leftarrow{\boldsymbol{r}}_{i}-\sum_{k\neq k^{*}}{\boldsymbol{r}}^{(k)}_{i}
.
E_{3}
is indistinguishable from
E_{2}
because the additive secret sharing is information-theoretically secure.

-

E_{4}
(Figure
16
): In
E_{4}
, instead of decrypting
{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{ev}}}^{(k^{*})}_{i}
,
{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{er}}}^{(k^{*})}_{i}
for
i\in\{i_{0},i_{1}\}
during
\mathsf{Mix}
,
{\boldsymbol{v}}^{(k^{*})}_{i}
,
{\boldsymbol{r}}^{(k^{*})}_{i}
are obtained by directly using their corresponding values in the
\mathsf{Enc}
call for
i_{0}/i_{1}
.
E_{4}
is indistinguishable from
E_{3}
because of correctness of decryption.

-

E_{5}
(Figure
17
): In
E_{5}
, instead of decrypting
{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{ev}}}^{(k^{*})}_{i}
,
{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{er}}}^{(k^{*})}_{i}
for
i\in[n]\setminus\{i_{0},i_{1}\}
during
\mathsf{Mix}
,
{\boldsymbol{v}}^{(k^{*})}_{i}
,
{\boldsymbol{r}}^{(k^{*})}_{i}
are obtained by rerunning the
\mathsf{Enc}
algorithm on input
{\boldsymbol{v}}_{i}
for sender
S_{i}
using the randomnesses from the random tape issued to
\mathcal{A}
.
E_{5}
is indistinguishable from
E_{4}
because of correctness of decryption.

-

E_{6}
(Figure
18
): In
E_{6}
, encrypted shares
{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{ev}}}^{(k^{*})}_{i_{0}},{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{er}}}^{(k^{*})}_{i_{0}},{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{ev}}}^{(k^{*})}_{i_{1}},{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{er}}}^{(k^{*})}_{i_{1}}
in the
\mathsf{Enc}
calls for
i_{0},i_{1}
are replaced by encryptions of
0
.
E_{6}
is indistinguishable from
E_{5}
by the IND-CPA security of
\mathsf{E}
.

-

E_{7}
(Figure
19
): In
E_{7}
, responses of
\mathcal{M}_{k}^{*}
in
\mathsf{E}_{\mathsf{EG}}^{\mathsf{th}}.\mathsf{TDec}
and
\mathsf{E}_{\mathsf{Pa}}^{\mathsf{th}}.\mathsf{TDec}
protocols are simulated by first obtaining the correct decryption
m
of the given ciphertext
c
using ideal decryption oracles
\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{E}_{\mathsf{EG}}^{\mathsf{th}}.\mathsf{TDec}}^{\mathsf{th}}
,
\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{E}_{\mathsf{Pa}}^{\mathsf{th}}.\mathsf{TDec}}^{\mathsf{th}}
and then simulating
\mathcal{M}_{k}^{*}
’s responses using
c,m
and secret keys for
(\mathcal{M}_{k})_{k\neq k^{*}}
.
E_{7}
is indistinguishable from
E_{6}
by the security of the threshold decryption protocols of
\mathsf{E}_{\mathsf{EG}}^{\mathsf{th}}
and
\mathsf{E}_{\mathsf{Pa}}^{\mathsf{th}}
. For
\mathsf{E}_{\mathsf{EG}}^{\mathsf{th}}
, it follows straightforwardly because given
c=(c_{0},c_{1})\in\mathbb{C}(\mathsf{E}_{\mathsf{EG}}^{\mathsf{th}})
and
m=c_{1}/\prod_{k\in[m]}c_{0}^{{\mathsf{sk}}^{(k)}_{\mathsf{EG}}}
obtained from
\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{E}_{\mathsf{EG}}^{\mathsf{th}}.\mathsf{TDec}}^{\mathsf{th}}
, the simulator can output
\frac{c_{1}}{m\prod\limits_{k\neq k^{*}}c_{0}^{{\mathsf{sk}}^{(k)}_{\mathsf{EG}}}}

=

\frac{c_{1}\prod\limits_{k\in[m]}c_{0}^{{\mathsf{sk}}^{(k)}_{\mathsf{EG}}}}{c_{1}\prod\limits_{k\neq k^{*}}c_{0}^{{\mathsf{sk}}^{(k)}_{\mathsf{EG}}}}

=

c_{0}^{{\mathsf{sk}}^{(k^{*})}_{\mathsf{EG}}}
, which is exactly the decryption share output by the real
\mathcal{M}_{k^{*}}
. For
\mathsf{E}_{\mathsf{Pa}}^{\mathsf{th}}
, Damgård et al. provide a proof (see Theorem 4;
(
Damgård et al. 2010
)
).

-

E_{8}
(Figure
20
): In
E_{8}
, instead of using
\boldsymbol{v}^{\prime}
given by
\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{E}_{\mathsf{Pa}}^{\mathsf{th}}.\mathsf{TDec}}^{\mathsf{th}}
during
\mathsf{Mix}
, it is set as
\boldsymbol{v}^{\prime}\leftarrow({\boldsymbol{v}}_{\pi(j)})_{j\in[n]}
. Here,
\pi\leftarrow{\pi}^{(m)}\circ\dots\circ{\pi}^{(1)}
is obtained using the experimenter-selected
{\pi}^{(k^{*})}
and
({\pi}^{(k)})_{k\neq k^{*}}
obtained from the random tape issued to
\mathcal{A}
,
({\boldsymbol{v}}_{i})_{i\in\{i_{0},i_{1}\}}
are obtained from their values in
\mathsf{Enc}
calls for
i_{0},i_{1}
, and
({\boldsymbol{v}}_{i})_{i\in[n]\setminus\{i_{0},i_{1}\}}
are obtained from
\mathcal{A}
’s input for senders
(S_{i})_{i\in[n]\setminus\{i_{0},i_{1}\}}
.
E_{8}
is indistinguishable from
E_{7}
by the correctness of
\mathsf{Shuffle}
and
\mathsf{E}_{\mathsf{Pa}}^{\mathsf{th}}.\mathsf{TDec}
protocols.

-

E_{9}
(Figure
21
): In
E_{9}
, instead of using
\boldsymbol{\tilde{\sigma}}
given by
\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{E}_{\mathsf{EG}}^{\mathsf{th}}.\mathsf{TDec}}^{\mathsf{th}}
during
{\mathsf{DB}\text{-}\mathsf{SM}}
, it is set as
\boldsymbol{\tilde{\sigma}}\leftarrow(({\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{\pi^{-1}(i)}^{\prime})^{{\boldsymbol{b}}_{i}})_{i\in[n]}
. Here
\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\prime}
denotes BB signatures sent by
\mathcal{A}
at the beginning of
{\mathsf{DB}\text{-}\mathsf{SM}}
,
\pi
is as obtained in
E_{8}
and
{\boldsymbol{b}}_{i}:=\sum_{k\in[m]}{\boldsymbol{b}}^{(k)}_{i}
is obtained using the experimenter-selected
{\boldsymbol{b}}^{(k^{*})}_{i}
and
({\boldsymbol{b}}^{(k)}_{i})_{k\neq k^{*}}
obtained from
\mathcal{A}
’s random tape.
E_{9}
is indistinguishable from
E_{8}
by the correctness of
\mathsf{Shuffle}
and
\mathsf{E}_{\mathsf{EG}}^{\mathsf{th}}.\mathsf{TDec}
protocols.

-

E_{10}
(Figure
22
): In
E_{10}
, instead of using
\boldsymbol{\tilde{S}}^{\prime},\boldsymbol{\tilde{c}}^{\prime\prime},\boldsymbol{\tilde{r}}^{\prime\prime}
given by
\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{E}_{\mathsf{EG}}^{\mathsf{th}}.\mathsf{TDec}}^{\mathsf{th}}
and
\mathcal{F}_{\mathsf{E}_{\mathsf{Pa}}^{\mathsf{th}}.\mathsf{TDec}}^{\mathsf{th}}
during
{\mathsf{DB}\text{-}\mathsf{RSM}}
, they are set as

\boldsymbol{\tilde{S}}^{\prime}

\leftarrow

({\boldsymbol{S}}_{\pi(j)}g_{1}^{{\boldsymbol{b_{S}}}_{j}})_{j\in[n]}
,
\boldsymbol{\tilde{c}}^{\prime\prime}

\leftarrow

({\boldsymbol{c}}_{\pi(j)}+{\boldsymbol{b_{c}}}_{j}^{\prime})_{j\in[n]}
,
\boldsymbol{\tilde{r}}^{\prime\prime}\leftarrow({\boldsymbol{\hat{r}}}_{\pi(j)}+{\boldsymbol{r}}_{\pi(j)}+{\boldsymbol{b_{r}}}_{j}^{\prime})_{j\in[n]}
. Here,
(\boldsymbol{S},\boldsymbol{c},\boldsymbol{\hat{r}})
denote BBS+ quasi-signatures sent by
\mathcal{A}
at the beginning of
{\mathsf{DB}\text{-}\mathsf{RSM}}
,
\pi
is as obtained in
E_{8}
,

{\boldsymbol{b_{S}}}_{j}\leftarrow\sum_{k\in[m]}{\boldsymbol{b_{S}}}^{(k)}_{j}
,
{\boldsymbol{b_{c}}}_{j}^{\prime}\leftarrow\sum_{k\in[m]}{\boldsymbol{b_{c}^{\prime}}}^{(k)}_{j}\bmod N
,

{\boldsymbol{b_{r}}}_{j}^{\prime}\leftarrow\sum_{k\in[m]}{\boldsymbol{b_{r}^{\prime}}}^{(k)}_{j}\bmod N
are obtained using experimenter-selected

{\boldsymbol{b_{S}}}^{(k^{*})}_{j},{\boldsymbol{b_{c}^{\prime}}}^{(k^{*})}_{j}:={\boldsymbol{b_{c}}}^{(k^{*})}_{j}+q{\boldsymbol{\chi_{c}}}^{(k^{*})}_{j},{\boldsymbol{b_{r}^{\prime}}}^{(k^{*})}_{j}:={\boldsymbol{b_{r}}}^{(k^{*})}_{j}+q{\boldsymbol{\chi_{r}}}^{(k^{*})}_{j}
and

({\boldsymbol{b_{S}}}^{(k)}_{j},{\boldsymbol{b_{c}^{\prime}}}^{(k)}_{j}:={\boldsymbol{b_{c}}}^{(k)}_{j}+q{\boldsymbol{\chi_{c}}}^{(k)}_{j},{\boldsymbol{b_{r}^{\prime}}}^{(k)}_{j}:={\boldsymbol{b_{r}}}^{(k)}_{j}+q{\boldsymbol{\chi_{r}}}^{(k)}_{j})_{k\neq k^{*}}
computed using
\mathcal{A}
’s random tape,

({\boldsymbol{r}}_{i})_{i\in\{i_{0},i_{1}\}}
are obtained from the
\mathsf{Enc}
calls for
i_{0},i_{1}
and
({\boldsymbol{r}}_{i})_{i\in[n]\setminus\{i_{0},i_{1}\}}
are obtained from the
random tape given to
\mathcal{A}
.
E_{10}
is indistinguishable from
E_{9}
by the correctness of
\mathsf{Shuffle}
,
\mathsf{E}_{\mathsf{EG}}^{\mathsf{th}}.\mathsf{TDec}
and
\mathsf{E}_{\mathsf{Pa}}^{\mathsf{th}}.\mathsf{TDec}
protocols.

-

E_{11}
(Figure
23
): In
E_{11}
, all encryptions/re-encryptions under
\mathsf{E}_{\mathsf{Pa}}^{\mathsf{th}}
are replaced by encryptions of
0
.
E_{11}
is indistinguishable to
E_{10}
by the IND-CPA security of
\mathsf{E}_{\mathsf{Pa}}^{\mathsf{th}}
under the DCR assumption
(
Paillier 1999
)
.

-

E_{12}
(Figure
24
): In
E_{12}
, all encryptions/re-encryptions under
\mathsf{E}_{\mathsf{EG}}^{\mathsf{th}}
are replaced by encryptions of
g_{1}^{0}
.
E_{12}
is indistinguishable to
E_{11}
by the IND-CPA security of
\mathsf{E}_{\mathsf{EG}}^{\mathsf{th}}
under the DDH assumption.

-

E_{13}
(Figure
25
): In
E_{13}
,
\mathcal{M}_{k^{*}}
’s responses during the DPKs in
{\mathsf{DB}\text{-}\mathsf{SM}}
are simulated using shares of
(\mathcal{M}_{k})_{k\neq k^{*}}
, as follows:

-

For
i\in\{i_{0},i_{1}\}
,
\mathcal{M}_{k^{*}}
’s responses are simulated completely if
\pi^{-1}(i_{0})\in J
; otherwise only the responses corresponding to the first equation
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{i}=g_{1}^{\sum_{k\in[m]}{\boldsymbol{v}}^{(k)}_{i}}h_{1}^{\sum_{k\in[m]}{\boldsymbol{r}}^{(k)}_{i}}
are simulated. For simulating the DPK completely,
({\boldsymbol{v}}^{(k)}_{i},{\boldsymbol{r}}^{(k)}_{i})_{k\neq k^{*}}
are obtained from their values in the
\mathsf{Enc}
calls for
i_{0}/i_{1}
and
({\boldsymbol{b}}^{(k)}_{i})_{k\neq k^{*}}
are chosen as the ones obtained in
E_{9}
. For simulating only the first component of the DPK,
\mathcal{M}_{k^{*}}
’s responses corresponding to the first equation are simulated using
({\boldsymbol{v}}^{(k)}_{i},{\boldsymbol{r}}^{(k)}_{i})_{k\neq k^{*}}
obtained from the
\mathsf{Enc}
calls for
i_{0}/i_{1}
, but
\mathcal{M}_{k^{*}}
’s strategy is followed for the second component except that a freshly sampled
{\boldsymbol{b}}^{(k^{*})}_{i}
is used instead.

-

For
i\in[n]\setminus\{i_{0},i_{1}\}
, the DPK is not simulated, i.e., values
{\boldsymbol{v}}^{(k^{*})}_{i},{\boldsymbol{r}}^{(k^{*})}_{i},{\boldsymbol{b}}^{(k^{*})}_{i}
are used exactly as
\mathcal{M}_{k^{*}}
does.

E_{13}
is indistinguishable from
E_{12}
because
a)
by the correctness of the blinded signatures, the DPK for
i_{0}
passes iff
j_{0}=\pi^{-1}(i_{0})\in J
(otherwise the blinded signature is invalid),
b)
by the assert condition in the
\mathsf{OTraceIn}
call, the DPK for
i_{1}
passes iff the DPK for
i_{0}
passes and
c)
even when these DPKs do not pass, their first component always passes.

-

E_{14}
(Figure
27
): In
E_{14}
,
\mathcal{M}_{k^{*}}
’s responses during the DPKs in
{\mathsf{DB}\text{-}\mathsf{RSM}}
are simulated using shares of
(\mathcal{M}_{k})_{k\neq k^{*}}
, as follows:

-

For
j\in\{j_{0},j_{1}\}
where
j_{0},j_{1}
are s.t.
v_{0}={\boldsymbol{v}}_{j_{0}}^{\prime}
and
v_{1}={\boldsymbol{v}}_{j_{1}}^{\prime}
, the DPK is simulated completely if
\pi(j_{0})\in I
; otherwise only its first two components are simulated. For simulating the DPK completely,
({\boldsymbol{b_{S}}}^{(k)}_{j},

{\boldsymbol{b_{c}}}^{(k)}_{j},

{\boldsymbol{b_{r}}}^{(k)}_{j})_{k\neq k^{*}}
are chosen as the ones obtained in
E_{10}
,
({\delta}^{(k)}_{0})_{k\neq k^{*}}
are obtained from the random tape issued to
\mathcal{A}
and
({\delta}^{(k)}_{1},{\delta}^{(k)}_{2})_{k\neq k^{*}}
are obtained by applying the
\mathsf{Mult}
algorithm to inputs
({\delta}^{(k)}_{0},{\boldsymbol{b_{S}}}^{(k)}_{j})
and
({\delta}^{(k)}_{0},{\boldsymbol{b_{c}}}^{(k)}_{j})
respectively. For simulating only the first two components,
\mathcal{M}_{k^{*}}
’s responses corresponding to the first two equations are simulated using
({\boldsymbol{b_{S}}}^{(k)}_{j},

{\boldsymbol{b_{c}}}^{(k)}_{j},{\delta}^{(k)}_{0},{\delta}^{(k)}_{1},{\delta}^{(k)}_{2})_{k\neq k^{*}}
obtained as above whereas for the third equation,
\mathcal{M}_{k^{*}}
’s strategy is followed except with a freshly sampled
{\boldsymbol{b_{r}}}^{(k^{*})}_{j}
.

-

For
j\in[n]\setminus\{j_{0},j_{1}\}
, the DPK is not simulated, i.e., values
({\boldsymbol{b_{S}}}^{(k^{*})}_{j},

{\boldsymbol{b_{c}}}^{(k^{*})}_{j},

{\boldsymbol{b_{r}}}^{(k^{*})}_{j},{\delta}^{(k^{*})}_{0},{\delta}^{(k^{*})}_{1},{\delta}^{(k^{*})}_{2})
are used exactly as
\mathcal{M}_{k^{*}}
does.

E_{14}
is indistinguishable from
E_{13}
because
a)
by the correctness of the blinded signatures, the DPK for
j_{0}
passes iff
i_{0}=\pi(j_{0})\in I
(otherwise the blinded signature is invalid),
b)
by the assert condition in the
\mathsf{OTraceOut}
call, the DPK for
j_{1}
passes iff the DPK for
j_{0}
passes, and
c)
even when these DPKs do not pass, their first two equations do pass.

-

E_{15}
(Figure
28
): In
E_{15}
,
{\mathfrak{z}}^{(k^{*})}_{1}
in
{\mathsf{DB}\text{-}\mathsf{RSM}}
is chosen uniformly at random from
\mathbb{Z}_{q}
.
E_{15}
is indistinguishable from
E_{14}
because of perfect blinding using
{\delta}^{(k^{*})}_{0}
.

-

E_{16}
(Figure
29
): In
E_{16}
, shares
{\boldsymbol{v}}^{(k^{*})}_{i_{0}},{\boldsymbol{r}}^{(k^{*})}_{i_{0}},{\boldsymbol{v}}^{(k^{*})}_{i_{1}},{\boldsymbol{r}}^{(k^{*})}_{i_{1}}
during the
\mathsf{Enc}
calls for
i_{0},i_{1}
are set as
0
.
E_{16}
is indistinguishable from
E_{15}
because these shares are not used anymore.

-

E_{17}
(Figure
30
): In
E_{17}
, commitments
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{i_{0}},{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{i_{1}}
in above
\mathsf{Enc}
calls commit
0
.
E_{17}
is indistinguishable from
E_{16}
because Pedersen commitments are perfectly hiding and the committed values are not used anywhere.

-

E_{18}
(Figure
31
): In
E_{18}
,
{\boldsymbol{\tilde{\sigma}}}_{i_{0}}
,
{\boldsymbol{\tilde{\sigma}}}_{i_{1}}
during
{\mathsf{DB}\text{-}\mathsf{SM}}
are replaced by randomly drawn elements from
\mathbb{G}_{1}
.
E_{18}
is indistinguishable from

E_{17}
because
{\boldsymbol{b}}^{(k^{*})}_{i_{0}},{\boldsymbol{b}}^{(k^{*})}_{i_{1}}
used in computing
{\boldsymbol{\tilde{\sigma}}}_{i_{0}}
,
{\boldsymbol{\tilde{\sigma}}}_{i_{1}}
are chosen uniformly at random from
\mathbb{Z}_{q}
.

-

E_{19}
(Figure
32
): In
E_{19}
,
{\boldsymbol{\tilde{S}}}_{j_{0}}^{\prime}
,
{\boldsymbol{\tilde{S}}}_{j_{1}}^{\prime}
during
{\mathsf{DB}\text{-}\mathsf{RSM}}
are replaced by randomly drawn elements from
\mathbb{G}_{1}
;
{\boldsymbol{\tilde{c}}}_{j_{0}}^{\prime\prime}
,
{\boldsymbol{\tilde{c}}}_{j_{1}}^{\prime\prime}
are computed as
{\boldsymbol{\tilde{c}}}_{j_{0}}^{\prime\prime\prime}+\sum_{k\neq k^{*}}{\boldsymbol{b_{c}^{\prime}}}^{(k)}_{j_{0}}
,
{\boldsymbol{\tilde{c}}}_{j_{1}}^{\prime\prime\prime}+\sum_{k\neq k^{*}}{\boldsymbol{b_{c}^{\prime}}}^{(k)}_{j_{1}}
where
{\boldsymbol{\tilde{c}}}_{j_{0}}^{\prime\prime\prime},{\boldsymbol{\tilde{c}}}_{j_{1}}^{\prime\prime\prime}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{q+q^{2}}
and
({\boldsymbol{b_{c}^{\prime}}}^{(k)}_{j_{0}},{\boldsymbol{b_{c}^{\prime}}}^{(k)}_{j_{1}})_{k\neq k^{*}}
are the ones obtained in
E_{10}
; and
{\boldsymbol{\tilde{r}}}_{j_{0}}^{\prime\prime}
,
{\boldsymbol{\tilde{r}}}_{j_{1}}^{\prime\prime}
are computed as
{\boldsymbol{\tilde{r}}}_{j_{0}}^{\prime\prime\prime}+\sum_{k\neq k^{*}}{\boldsymbol{b_{r}^{\prime}}}^{(k)}_{j_{0}},{\boldsymbol{\tilde{r}}}_{j_{1}}^{\prime\prime\prime}+\sum_{k\neq k^{*}}{\boldsymbol{b_{r}^{\prime}}}^{(k)}_{j_{1}}
where
{\boldsymbol{\tilde{r}}}_{j_{0}}^{\prime\prime\prime},{\boldsymbol{\tilde{r}}}_{j_{1}}^{\prime\prime\prime}\xleftarrow{\$}\mathbb{Z}_{2q+q^{2}}
and
({\boldsymbol{b_{r}^{\prime}}}^{(k)}_{j_{0}},{\boldsymbol{b_{r}^{\prime}}}^{(k)}_{j_{1}})_{k\neq k^{*}}
are the ones obtained in
E_{10}
.
E_{19}
is indistinguishable from
E_{18}
because
{\boldsymbol{b_{S}}}^{(k^{*})}_{j_{0}},{\boldsymbol{b_{S}}}^{(k^{*})}_{j_{1}}
used in computing
{\boldsymbol{\tilde{S}}}_{j_{0}}^{\prime}
,
{\boldsymbol{\tilde{S}}}_{j_{1}}^{\prime}
are chosen uniformly at random from
\mathbb{Z}_{q}
and
{\boldsymbol{b_{c}^{\prime}}}^{(k^{*})}_{j_{0}},{\boldsymbol{b_{c}^{\prime}}}^{(k^{*})}_{j_{1}}
(resp.
{\boldsymbol{b_{r}^{\prime}}}^{(k^{*})}_{j_{0}},{\boldsymbol{b_{r}^{\prime}}}^{(k^{*})}_{j_{1}}
) used in computing
{\boldsymbol{\tilde{c}}}_{j_{0}}^{\prime\prime}
,
{\boldsymbol{\tilde{c}}}_{j_{1}}^{\prime\prime}
(resp.
{\boldsymbol{\tilde{r}}}_{j_{0}}^{\prime\prime}
,
{\boldsymbol{\tilde{r}}}_{j_{1}}^{\prime\prime}
) are chosen uniformly at random from an exponentially larger space than the corresponding messages
{\boldsymbol{c}}_{\pi(j_{0})},{\boldsymbol{c}}_{\pi(j_{1})}
(resp.

{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{r}}}_{\pi(j_{0})},{\boldsymbol{\mathsf{r}}}_{\pi(j_{1})}
).

In
E_{19}
,
\mathcal{A}
’s view is identical for both
b=0
and
b=1
because in this experiment only
\boldsymbol{v}^{\prime}
sent at the end of the
\mathsf{Mix}
protocol depends on
v_{i_{0}},v_{i_{1}}
and
\boldsymbol{v}^{\prime}
is identically distributed for any choice of
b
because
{\pi}^{(k^{*})}
is uniformly distributed over
\mathsf{Perm}(n)
. Thus, by a standard hybrid argument, the theorem holds.

C.4.
Proof for Theorem
4

In this case, the proof proceeds exactly as above except that in experiment
E_{13}
, DPKs for both
i_{0},i_{1}
are always simulated completely and in experiment
E_{14}
, DPKs for both
j_{0},j_{1}
are always simulated completely.
E_{13}
and
E_{14}
are indistinguishable from their previous experiments because in this case, even though
\mathcal{A}
is not restricted by the constraints at
\mathsf{OTraceIn}/\mathsf{OTraceOut}
calls,
\mathcal{A}
does not control
\mathcal{Q}
anymore and thus does not even learn whether a DPK passed or not by the property of the DPKs (see Section
3.1.5
).

C.5.
Secrecy in the malicious model

In this section, we sketch a proof that when the additional steps of Appendix
B
are applied then our construction protects secrecy (Definition
4
) even against general malicious adversaries. We assume that secure MPC protocols are used for Beaver triples generation and for
\mathsf{E}_{\mathsf{EG}}^{\mathsf{th}}
and
\mathsf{E}_{\mathsf{Pa}}^{\mathsf{th}}
distributed key generation.

The proof essentially follows the structure of the proof in Appendix
C.3
with the following differences:

-

In
E_{5}
,
{\boldsymbol{v}}^{(k^{*})}_{i}
,
{\boldsymbol{r}}^{(k^{*})}_{i}
for
i\in[n]\setminus\{i_{0},i_{1}\}
are obtained by extracting them from proofs
({\boldsymbol{\rho}}^{(k)}_{\mathsf{ev}_{i}},{\boldsymbol{\rho}}^{(k)}_{\mathsf{er}_{i}})_{k\in[m]}
; the corresponding proofs for
i\in\{i_{0},i_{1}\}
are simulated.
E_{5}
is indistinguishable from
E_{4}
by knowledge soundness of
{\boldsymbol{\rho}}^{(k)}_{\mathsf{ev}_{i}},{\boldsymbol{\rho}}^{(k)}_{\mathsf{er}_{i}}
for
i\in[n]\setminus\{i_{0},i_{1}\}
and their zero-knowledgeness for
i\in\{i_{0},i_{1}\}
.

-

In
E_{8}
,
({\pi}^{(k)})_{k\neq k^{*}}
are obtained by extracting them from proofs of shuffle produced by
(\mathcal{M}_{k})_{k\neq k^{*}}
and
({\boldsymbol{v}}_{i})_{i\in[n]\setminus\{i_{0},i_{1}\}}
are obtained by extracting them from proofs
{\boldsymbol{\rho}}_{\epsilon_{i}}
for
i\in[n]\setminus\{i_{0},i_{1}\}
. Proofs of shuffle for
\mathcal{M}_{k^{*}}
and proofs
{\boldsymbol{\rho}}_{\epsilon_{i}}
for
i\in\{i_{0},i_{1}\}
are simulated.
E_{8}
is indistinguishable to
E_{7}
by
a)
knowledge soundness of proofs of shuffles for
(\mathcal{M}_{k})_{k\neq k^{*}}
,
b)
knowledge soundness of
{\boldsymbol{\rho}}_{\epsilon_{i}}
for
i\in[n]\setminus\{i_{0},i_{1}\}
,
c)
zero-knowledgeness of proofs of shuffle for
\mathcal{M}_{k^{*}}
,
d)
zero-knowledgeness of
{\boldsymbol{\rho}}_{\epsilon_{i}}
for
i\in\{i_{0},i_{1}\}
, and
e)
correctness of threshold decryption protocol
\mathsf{E}_{\mathsf{Pa}}^{\mathsf{th}}.\mathsf{TDec}
.

-

In
E_{9}
,
({\boldsymbol{b}}^{(k)}_{i})_{k\neq k^{*}}
are obtained by extracting them from
({\boldsymbol{\rho}}^{(k)}_{\tilde{\sigma}_{i}})_{k\neq k^{*}}
. The corresponding proofs for
k=k^{*}
are simulated.
E_{9}
is indistinguishable from
E_{8}
by
a)
verification of correct construction of
{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}_{\sigma}^{\prime}
from
\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\prime}
,
b)
knowledge soundness of proofs of shuffle that prove that each
(\mathcal{M}_{k})_{k\neq k^{*}}
shuffled encrypted signatures in this phase correctly using the inverse of the permutation
{\pi}^{(k)}
it applied during mixing,
c)
knowledge soundness of
({\boldsymbol{\rho}}^{(k)}_{\tilde{\sigma}_{i}})_{k\neq k^{*}}
,
d)
zero-knowledgeness of proofs of shuffle for
k=k^{*}
,
e)
zero-knowledgeness of
{\boldsymbol{\rho}}^{(k^{*})}_{\tilde{\sigma}_{i}}
, and
f)
correctness of threshold decryption protocol
\mathsf{E}_{\mathsf{EG}}^{\mathsf{th}}.\mathsf{TDec}
.

-

In
E_{10}
,
({\boldsymbol{b_{S}}}^{(k)}_{j},{\boldsymbol{b_{c}^{\prime}}}^{(k)}_{j},{\boldsymbol{b_{r}^{\prime}}}^{(k)}_{j})_{k\neq k^{*}}
are obtained by extracting them from
({\boldsymbol{\rho}}^{(k)}_{bS_{j}},

{\boldsymbol{\rho}}^{(k)}_{bc_{j}},

{\boldsymbol{\rho}}^{(k)}_{br_{j}})
respectively and
{\boldsymbol{r}}_{\pi(j)}
is obtained by extracting them from
{\boldsymbol{\rho}}_{\epsilon_{r_{\pi(j)}}}
. Proofs
({\boldsymbol{\rho}}^{(k^{*})}_{bS_{j}},

{\boldsymbol{\rho}}^{(k^{*})}_{bc_{j}},

{\boldsymbol{\rho}}^{(k^{*})}_{br_{j}})
and
{\boldsymbol{\rho}}_{\epsilon_{r_{i_{0}}}},{\boldsymbol{\rho}}_{\epsilon_{r_{i_{1}}}}
are simulated.
E_{10}
is indistinguishable from
E_{9}
by
a)
verification of correct construction of
(\boldsymbol{\epsilon_{S}},\boldsymbol{\epsilon_{c}},\boldsymbol{\epsilon_{\hat{r}}})
from
(\boldsymbol{S},\boldsymbol{c},\boldsymbol{\hat{r}})
,
b)
knowledge soundness of the proofs of shuffle that prove that each
\mathcal{M}_{k}
shuffled encrypted signatures in this phase correctly using
{\pi}^{(k)}
applied during mixing,
c)
knowledge soundness of
({\boldsymbol{\rho}}^{(k)}_{bS_{j}},

{\boldsymbol{\rho}}^{(k)}_{bc_{j}},

{\boldsymbol{\rho}}^{(k)}_{br_{j}})
,
d)
zero-knowledgeness of proofs of shuffle for
k=k^{*}
,
e)
zero-knowledgeness of
{\boldsymbol{\rho}}^{(k^{*})}_{bc_{j}},

{\boldsymbol{\rho}}^{(k^{*})}_{br_{j}}
and
{\boldsymbol{\rho}}_{\epsilon_{r_{i_{0}}}},{\boldsymbol{\rho}}_{\epsilon_{r_{i_{1}}}}
, and
f)
correctness of threshold decryption protocols
\mathsf{E}_{\mathsf{EG}}^{\mathsf{th}}.\mathsf{TDec}
and
\mathsf{E}_{\mathsf{Pa}}^{\mathsf{th}}.\mathsf{TDec}
.

-

In
E_{13}
, the correctness of blinded signatures is ensured through the additional verification that signatures
{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{j}^{\prime}
for each
j\in J
are valid BB signatures on
{\boldsymbol{v}}_{j}^{\prime}
and are invalid signatures for
j\not\in J
.

-

In
E_{14}
, the correctness of blinded signatures is ensured through the additional verification that quasi-signatures
({\boldsymbol{S}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{c}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{\hat{r}}}_{i})
for each
i\in I
are valid BBS+ quasi signatures using commitment
{\boldsymbol{\gamma}}_{i}
and invalid for
i\not\in I
. Note that correctness of quasi-signatures implies correctness of full BBS+ signatures for
i\in\{i_{0},i_{1}\}
because encryptions
{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}_{r_{i}}
are generated by honest senders. Further, in this case,
({\delta}^{(k)}_{0},{\delta}^{(k)}_{1},{\delta}^{(k)}_{2})_{k\neq k^{*}}
are obtained by extracting
{\delta}^{(k)}_{0}
from proofs of knowledge of the opening of
({\mathfrak{z}}^{(k)}_{1})_{k\neq k^{*}}
and computing
({\delta}^{(k)}_{1},{\delta}^{(k)}_{2})_{k\neq k^{*}}
using the
\mathsf{Mult}
algorithm on inputs
({\delta}^{(k)}_{0},{\boldsymbol{b_{S}}}^{(k)}_{j})
and
({\delta}^{(k)}_{0},{\boldsymbol{b_{c}}}^{(k)}_{j})
respectively, where
{\boldsymbol{b_{S}}}^{(k)}_{j},{\boldsymbol{b_{c}}}^{(k)}_{j}:=({\boldsymbol{b_{c}^{\prime}}}^{(k)}_{j}\bmod q)
are the ones obtained in
E_{10}
(note that this requires Beaver triples held by
(\mathcal{M}_{k})_{k\neq k^{*}}
which are provided by the ideal functionality for the MPC protocol for Beaver triple generation).

Appendix D
Privacy risk analysis of TraceIn/TraceOut queries

In this section, we provide formal rules to analyse the privacy risk impact of allowing a given set of TraceIn/TraceOut queries to a querier in an application. Since the goal of the analysis is to apriori decide whether to allow the queries or not, we assume that we only have query inputs and not their outputs. Further, we assume that no query’s input depends on other queries’ outputs. Given this, we adopt a static analysis approach where we conservatively estimate the information
potentially
leaked by the queries. If the actual information leaked by a query depends on its output, we conservatively assume that the query potentially leaks the information corresponding to both the outputs.

Consider a mixnet with input ciphertexts
({\boldsymbol{c}}_{i})_{i\in[n]}
and output plaintexts
({\boldsymbol{v}}_{j}^{\prime})_{j\in[n]}
. Let
Q
denote the set of proposed allowed queries containing elements of the form
(\mathsf{TraceIn},

i,

J)
and
(\mathsf{TraceOut},

I,

j)
for
i,j\in[n]
and
I,J\subseteq[n]
representing the TraceIn(
i,J
) and TraceOut(
I,j
) queries respectively (
\mathsf{BTraceIn}
/
\mathsf{BTraceOut}
queries can be compiled to this format). Let
K(i,J)
denote the fact that the querier potentially knows that ciphertext
{\boldsymbol{c}}_{i}
encrypts a plaintext in set
{\boldsymbol{v}}_{J}^{\prime}
. Let
K(I,j)
(with an index set as the first argument and an index as the second argument) denote the fact that the querier potentially knows that plaintext
{\boldsymbol{v}}_{j}^{\prime}
is encrypted in some ciphertext in set
{\boldsymbol{c}}_{I}
. We therefore have the following rules:

-

R0.1:

\forall i\in[n]:K(i,[n])
.

-

R0.2:

\forall j\in[n]:K([n],j)
.

-

R1.1:

\forall i\in[n],J\subseteq[n]:

(\mathsf{TraceIn},i,J)\in Q

\implies

K(i,J)

\wedge

K(i,[n]\setminus J)
.

-

R1.2:

\forall j\in[n],I\subseteq[n]:

(\mathsf{TraceOut},I,j)\in Q

\implies

K(I,j)

\wedge

K([n]\setminus I,j)
.

-

R2.1:

\forall i\in[n],J,J^{\prime}\subseteq[n]:K(i,J)\wedge K(i,J^{\prime})\implies K(i,J\cap J^{\prime})
.

-

R2.2:

\forall j\in[n],I,I^{\prime}\subseteq[n]:K(I,j)\wedge K(I^{\prime},j)\implies K(I\cap I^{\prime},j)
.

-

R3.1:

\forall i,j\in[n],I,J\subseteq[n]:K(i,J)\wedge K(I,j)\wedge i\not\in I\wedge j\in J\implies K(i,J\setminus\{j\})
.

-

R3.2:

\forall i,j\in[n],I,J\subseteq[n]:K(i,J)\wedge K(I,j)\wedge i\in I\wedge j\not\in J\implies K(I\setminus\{i\},j)
.

Rules R0.1 and R0.2 encode the fact that the querier initially knows that each input ciphertext encrypts a plaintext in the output list and each output plaintext is encrypted in a ciphertext in the input list. R1.1 (symmetrically R1.2) encodes that a TraceIn(
i,J
) query leads to either the querier learning that
{\boldsymbol{c}}_{i}
encrypts a plaintext in
{\boldsymbol{v}}_{J}^{\prime}
(if the query passed) or that
{\boldsymbol{c}}_{i}
encrypts a plaintext in
{\boldsymbol{v}}_{[n]\setminus J}^{\prime}
(if the query failed). Thus, potentially, the querier may learn both. R2.1 (symmetrically R2.2) encodes that if the querier knows that
{\boldsymbol{c}}_{i}
encrypts a value in
{\boldsymbol{v}}_{J}^{\prime}
and in
{\boldsymbol{v}}_{J^{\prime}}^{\prime}
then it knows that
{\boldsymbol{c}}_{i}
encrypts a value in
{\boldsymbol{v}}_{J\cap J^{\prime}}^{\prime}
. R3.1 (symmetrically R3.2) encodes that if the querier knows that
{\boldsymbol{c}}_{i}
encrypts a value in
{\boldsymbol{v}}_{J}^{\prime}
but there exists a
j\in J
that is known to be encrypted in a ciphertext in a set
I
that does not include
i
, then it knows that
{\boldsymbol{c}}_{i}
cannot encrypt
{\boldsymbol{v}}_{j}^{\prime}
because of distinctness of encrypted values.

Given these rules, the goal of our analysis is to output
a)
for each
i\in[n]
, the smallest set
J_{\text{min}_{i}}
such that
K(i,J_{\text{min}_{i}})
holds; and
b)
for each
j\in[n]
, the smallest set
I_{\text{min}_{j}}
such that
K(I_{\text{min}_{j}},i)
holds. Note that if for some
i
, no TraceIn(
i,J
) query exists in
Q
then
J_{\text{min}_{i}}=[n]
(similarly for
I_{\text{min}_{j}}
). The
J_{\text{min}},I_{\text{min}}
outputs tell exactly what information is potentially leaked by the queries in
Q
. This information can directly feed to the application-level security analysis. Since each implication rule monotonically decreases the size of either the
J
set for
K(i,J)
or the
I
set for
K(I,j)
,
J_{\mathsf{min}_{i}}
and
I_{\mathsf{min}_{j}}
can both be obtained in finite time for a finite set
Q
using a fixed-point algorithm.
